Marcus lifted a sword out of the chest, a short legion blade in a split scabbard, and drew it a hand's breadth, and looked at the bloom of rust along the edge, and slid it home. "The dead don't care what a man was sent down for. When they come over the side I'd like him to have something sharper than his opinion."

"And if he comes over the side at *you*? With your iron?"

"Then he'll have done the king's work for him and saved the hangman a rope."

His hands, holding the sword, were shaking so badly that the scabbard chattered against the hilt. He set it down on the lid of the chest so that he would not have to listen to it.

Roderic looked at the hands, and then away, out over the ice, as if something there needed his attention.

"A mob with swords is a mob," the General said at last. "It's worse than a mob without. If you mean to do this, do it properly. Sort them first. Then arm them. Sorted men are a cohort. Unsorted, they're a riot with a boat under it."

"Then sort them."

"*I'll* sort them?"

"You've sorted more men than I've met." Marcus got up. He swayed as he did it, and caught himself on the chest, and saw Roderic's hand come out from his side, quick as a striking adder, and stop, and go back. "Show me how."

The old man looked at him for a long moment. Then he grunted, and unfolded his arms, and turned to the waist, and filled his chest, and Marcus heard the voice come up out of him that had carried across the Long Field thirty years ago and had never in his life needed a herald.

"*On your feet! All of you! Stand up, you sick dogs, stand up and come aft, in your tens, the way they chained you! Move!*"

---

It took the whole of the morning.

Roderic did it the way he would have sorted remounts at a horse fair: by walking down the line and looking, and looking again, and saying very little. He made every man hold out his hands, palms up, and he read them the way a priest reads a liver. *Oar. Oar. Plough. Hammer, that one. Nothing, you've never worked a day.* A man with soft palms and an inky middle finger was a clerk, and a clerk could keep a tally. A man with a hard ridge across the inside of his fingers had held reins, and Roderic stopped in front of him and looked at him a long time.

"Horses?"

"Other men's, General," said the man, who was lean and bandy and missing his front teeth. "Tullus. I'm a horse-thief, I won't pretend different. But I never stole a horse I didn't treat better than its master."

"If we find a horse on Cyrene," Roderic said, "you're mine."

Marcus walked behind him and did the other half. He had learned it from Tiberius, a long time ago, on a beach, the summer they gave him his first vine-stick. *Look at their hands and you'll know what they've done,* Tiberius had said. *Look at their eyes and you'll know what they'll do.* So he looked at their eyes, and asked each man the same question, the one Tiberius had asked him.

"What can you do that I can't?"

Most of them did not know how to answer. Some said nothing. Some said *nothing, Highness,* and meant it. A few laughed, and he let them, and waited.

The giant baker with one eye said, "Bread, Highness." And then, as if it had cost him: "Oppius. Of Bread Street. I can make bread out of nothing. I've done it with chalk." Marcus looked at the stores in the waist, the casks of weevilled biscuit and the barrel of salt pork that had been green when they loaded it, and gave him the stores. Oppius stood holding the key to the bread-locker in his great white hand as though he had been handed a relic.

A thin grey man with a stoop and very clean hands said he was a barber. "Hostus, Highness. Barber and tooth-drawer, Tanner's Lane. I've stitched more scalps than any surgeon in the legion, and cheaper." He had gone to the prison for debt, he said, after a magistrate's son died under his knife of a rotten tooth he had told the boy's father to have out a year before. "The father didn't care for my opinion. He cared less for the bill." Marcus gave him the surgeon's chest, such as it was: a roll of linen, a jar of vinegar, a curved needle and a hank of gut. Hostus looked at it as a carpenter looks at a blunt saw, and sighed, and said he had worked with worse.

A shepherd from the hill country of Vessa, brown and silent, with a leather sling knotted around his waist for a belt, said he could throw. Roderic snorted. The shepherd did not argue. He unknotted the sling and put a rivet from the arms chest in the pouch and turned and whirled it once, twice, with a short flat crack like a whip, and out on the sea, forty paces off, a slab of ice the size of a tabletop split in two along a line Marcus could have drawn with a ruler.

"Atticus, Highness," the shepherd said. "The magistrate's bailiff took my flock for the tax. He came back for my daughter. I hit him from sixty paces." He wound the sling round his waist again. "They said at the trial it was a lucky throw."

"Find me every man aboard who can throw like that," said Marcus.

"There's none can throw like that, Highness. There's maybe a dozen I can teach to throw well enough."

"Then teach them."

A young man, black-haired, with a broken knuckle that had set crooked and the burned, salt-cracked forearms of a sailor, came out of the seventh ten when Marcus reached it and stood in front of him without being asked.

"Iason," he said. "Of Cyrene."

Marcus stopped.

"I was apprenticed in the yards," said the young man. His voice had the burr of the southern isles in it, the flat vowels Marcus had heard in the harbor taverns from shipwrights and caulkers come up to the capital for the season's pay. "Master Philon's yard, on the third slip. He sent me up to the capital with a hull for the Lord Marshal's fleet and I put a knife into a chandler who sold us rotten cordage and called it good. I've been in the dark since spring." His eyes were very bright. "My mother's in Cyrene, Highness. And my sisters, two. The fort fell thirteen days ago, and nobody in the prison could tell me anything, and nobody on this ship knows anything either. But I know the town. I know every slip and every lane and every rock in the harbor. I know where the Red garrison will have put its watch, because there's only four places worth putting it. I know where the fort's water comes from. Whatever you need to know about Cyrene, I know it. Only take me ashore."

"You were coming ashore anyway."

"In the first boat," said Iason. "Highness. I want the first boat."

Marcus looked at him for a moment. Then he took a sword out of the chest and held it out, hilt first.

"Sit with the captain tonight," he said. "Tell him everything you know about the harbor. Then come and tell me everything you know about the fort."

He went down the line. A thief named Spurius Lento, whom the others called Fingers, who could open any lock on the ship and showed them by opening the bread-locker while Oppius was still holding its key. A cooper with a broken nose, Septimus Calvo, who owed the Silk Row eleven crowns and asked if the prince was keeping a ledger, and Marcus said he was. An old debtor, Fabius, white-stubbled and toothless, who could neither lift a shield nor draw a sling, and stood at the end of his ten holding out his empty hands as though apologizing for them. The drum-boy, Kaeso, who was fourteen and in the prison in his father's place because his father had the cough and would not have lasted the winter in the dark. Roderic stopped in front of the boy and looked at him for a long time and said nothing at all, and when he walked on, Marcus saw the General's jaw working.

And at the end of it, when the sun, wherever it was, had gone past noon, they had what Roderic called a cohort and Marcus privately called a hope.

Two centuries of shields under Rufo, eighty men, the ones the optio had chosen himself out of the whole ship, and not the biggest. *The ones who won't put it down, Highness. I know the look. I've seen it in the glass.* A dozen slingers under the shepherd. A gang of tradesmen under Calvo, carpenters and coopers and a smith, who would build what needed building and break what needed breaking. Lento's hands for the locks. Iason's head for the town. Hostus for the dying, and Oppius for the living. And the rest with spears, a long ragged swarm of thieves and debtors and deserters and brawlers who had been nothing at dawn and were now, at least, the spear-men of the penal cohort, which was not much, but was something with a name.

"Two centuries and a rabble," Roderic said, leaning on the rail beside him, watching Rufo shove men into a line. His voice was gruff. "It's more than we had at breakfast."

"Every man who takes a blade gives me his name." Marcus raised his voice so the waist would hear it. It cracked, and he raised it anyway. "His own. Not the one he gave the magistrate. I'm not writing them for any court. I'm writing them so that if you die on this ship, I'll know what to call you."

So they came to the chest, one at a time, and he gave each man his blade and said each man's name back to him, once, as if fixing it. His hands shook. He did not drop a single one.

Roderic watched him do it. When it was done, and the chest was empty, and the last of the cohort had gone back to the benches with rust on his palms and his name in the prince's mouth, the old man said, not looking at him:

"Where did you learn to sort men like that?"

"Tiberius."

The General said nothing for a moment. Something passed over his face, quick, like the shadow of a gull crossing a field, and was gone.

"He was good at it," Roderic said, and went forward.

Marcus stayed by the chest, and found, without having meant to look, that he was looking at the foot of the afterdeck ladder.

Alana was there. She had been there, he understood, for some time. She stood on the bottom rung in that great borrowed sea-cloak with the hood back and the violet hem gone black with wet, and she had her book open against her forearm, and the pen in her hand, and she was not writing. She was only watching. Her face in the cold had gone very pale, and the tip of her nose was red, and there was a smear of ink, as there always was, along the line of her jaw, under the ear, where she had pushed back her hair.

He remembered, quite suddenly, the gardens.

Not her. That was the shameful thing. He did not remember her face from that summer at all. He remembered a voice at his elbow, a girl's voice, quick and bright, talking about the stars, about the canon of the stars and some error in it, and a book under somebody's arm, and the smell of the lime trees, and himself laughing. He remembered laughing. He did not remember why.

And he remembered the high table. The sonnet. He remembered that very well. He remembered the lines on the paper, written in a careful, beautiful hand, recopied, he could see now, more than once, and how he had held them up to the candles and done her voice. He remembered the roar. He remembered being pleased with the roar.

*Fourteen lines,* he thought. *She wrote fourteen lines and recopied them for the hand, and I used them to get a laugh from Varro's table.*

She shut the book. She went up the ladder without looking at him, and was gone.

He thought of a dozen things he might have said. He thought of them all the rest of that afternoon, while the ice rang off the bows and the cohort learned the names of its officers and Atticus's slingers broke floes for practice out on the grey sea. He turned them over and over, the way a man turns coins in a purse that he knows will not cover the debt.

None of them were any good. He did not say any of them.

---

**ALANA**

In the afternoon the sea went soft.

There was no other word for it. She had been watching the ice for an hour from the afterdeck, with the weather-book under her cloak, and she had seen it change. The floes had been hard and white all morning, ringing off the poles like wood. Now they were grey, and pitted, and rotten. They had a look she knew from the margins of old books left too near a damp wall: honeycombed, crumbling, eaten away from underneath. When the bow-men struck them with their poles they did not ring. They broke, softly, like wet bread.

And there was a seam in the sea.

It ran across their course a quarter-mile ahead, as straight and plain as a line ruled on a slate. On the near side of it the water was slate-grey and still, crowded with ice. On the far side it was black, and clear of ice entirely, and it *smoked*. A thin white breath stood up off it, knee-high, and leaned with the wind, and where the seam met the cold water the smoke thickened into a low rolling bank, like a hedge of wool laid along the sea.

She went down the ladder to the steering oar.

"Captain."

Nerva did not take his eyes off the seam. "Lady."

"Not a lady. What is that?"

He was silent long enough that she thought he would not answer. Then he said, "Cyrene's breath. Or one finger of it." He nodded at the black water beyond the line. "The spring's under the island. But the warm water doesn't stay under the island. It runs out under the sea, along the old fire-roads in the rock, the way water runs along a gutter. Miles, sometimes. The shipwrights say the island has fingers, and it reaches out with them. Where the finger comes up near the top, the sea goes warm, and the ice rots from below and goes soft, and the sea smokes. That's the start of the Grey, lady. Out here. Long before you see the wall."

She had her book out. Her fingers were stiff, and she did not care. "And the danger?"

"Oh, there's plenty." He spoke the way other men spoke of the weather at home, with a kind of weary fondness. "First is the ice. Ice that's rotted from below has no keel left. It sits on the water heavy at the top and light at the bottom, and one day it rolls. No wind. No warning. A slab of it the size of a barn turns over in the sea like a man in his sleep, and if you're beside it, it takes you under with it." He nodded at the bow-men. "That's why I've the poles out. Not to fend. To keep her off them."

She wrote: *Rotten ice. Rolls without warning.*

"Second is the seam itself. Warm water's lighter than cold. Where they meet, they don't mix. They slide. The warm goes over the top and the cold goes under it, and they run past each other like two rivers in one bed, the top one going one way and the bottom one going the other. Put a ship across the seam and her keel's in one river and her oars are in the other." He shifted the steering oar a hand's breadth. "She'll spin. She'll set sideways a mile in an hour and the man at the helm won't feel it. And the fog comes up along the line like a wall, so you can't see the sun to know it. Half the wrecks in the southern isles are ships that crossed a seam and thought they were somewhere else."

*Seam: warm water over cold, running two ways. Ships set sideways unseen.*

"And the needle," she said. "In the Grey."

"Lies." He said it flatly. "The rock's iron, under the islands. Black rock, heavy, fire-born. A needle near an island swings to it and points at the rock instead of the north. I've seen a needle on Cyrene's banks spin a whole circle in a cable's length. Only fools and Tharsians sail by the needle here." He glanced at her book, and something like the ghost of a smile moved in his white beard. "And there's the boils. You'll want those. Scholars always want the boils."

"What are the boils?"

He lifted his chin forward, at the black smoking water past the seam. "Watch."

She watched.

For a long time there was nothing but the smoke, leaning. Then, a cable to starboard of their course, perhaps two hundred paces off, the sea began to move.

It began as a trembling. A patch of water the size of a threshing-floor went dull, and then pale, and then it *heaved*, the way a pot heaves before it boils, and broke all over its surface into bubbles, great and small, seething, rising, bursting, so that the whole patch of sea turned white and roared softly like rain on a lake. The stink came across to them a moment later. Rotten eggs. Struck matches. Something hot and sour and mineral underneath it, the smell of the bottom of the world. Steam stood up off the boil in a single white column, straight as a tree in the still air, and things came up in it and lay on the surface and turned slowly in the seething water. Fish. Dozens of them. Silver and white, and every one of them dead, every one of them *cooked*.

"Fire-vent under the bank," said Nerva. "It opens when it likes, breathes for a while, and shuts again. If you're over it when it opens, lady, the water under you goes to froth, and froth won't hold a ship up. She drops. Like a cart going through a rotten bridge. I saw a fishing smack go into a boil off Cyrene headland when I was a boy. One moment she was there. The next she was gone, and there was only white water, and then not even that, and the fish coming up cooked."

Alana did not write anything for a moment.

"That's why I take her through by the smell," the captain said. "Not by the stones. The stones would tell me where the island is. They'd not tell me where the island is *breathing*." He put the steering oar over. The *Clemency* came round, very slowly, groaning, away from the boil. "Now. Hold on to something, lady. We're crossing the seam."

She held on.

The bow went over the line into the black water, and the smoke closed round it, and she felt the ship *change*. It was as if a hand had taken the old hulk by the keel and turned her. The deck tilted under Alana's feet, not with a wave, with no wave at all, and the stern swung, and the ice on the near side of the line slid past them sideways like a crowd parting. Pell, beside the captain, swore. Nerva said nothing. He leaned on the steering oar with his whole long body and held it there, his chafed wrists white on the loom, and the *Clemency* hung across the seam for one long, sick, sliding moment with her bow in one river and her stern in the other, and then shook herself, like a dog coming out of a pond, and went on.

The warm air came over them. Alana felt it on her face. It smelled of sulphur and wet stone and, very faintly, of something burned.

"There," said Nerva. "Now we're in her breath. We'll see the wall by dark."

Alana looked down at the page of her book. Her hand had written, quite without her, in the margin, very small: *The island has fingers. It reaches out with them.*

She looked at it for a while. Then she did not cross it out.

---

He found her at the stern rail an hour before dark.

She had known he would. She had been watching the smoke lean on the black water, and the light going out of the pewter sky, and the long dark line along the southern horizon that she had at first taken for a bank of cloud and now knew was not cloud. It stood up off the sea like a wall of wet wool, the way the Grey had stood off the moles at home, but higher, and yellower at its foot, and it did not move.

"Cyrene," said Marcus, beside her.

She did not look round. "Cyrene's Grey. We will not see Cyrene. One never sees the island. One sees the fog the island makes." She heard herself, the lecturing note, and could not stop it. "Every island breathes. The water under it is warm and the sea around it is cold, and where they meet, the sea smokes. It is the same at home. It is the same at Corvus." She stopped. "It was."

"Nerva says we'll be in it by the first watch."

"Then you have not come to talk to me about fog."

He was quiet a moment. She could feel him there, at the edge of her eye, a tall darkness in a sodden cloak smelling of sweat and rust and something sour under both, the sweat of a body that wants a drink and has been told no. He had put his hands on the rail. She could hear them. A very small sound, the bones of his fingers knocking on the wood.

"Tell me about the dead," he said.

"You have seen them."

"I've *fought* them." His voice was low and very flat. "That's different. I've seen them walk up out of a river and I've seen them come over a wall, and I know they don't stop when you cut them, and I know what it does to a line when the man in front of you was in your tent last week. I don't know what they are. I don't know what they want. I don't know how to kill one so it stays killed, and you do. I've watched you count those casks. You count them like a woman who knows how much she'll need." He paused. "So tell me. Please."

*Please.*

She turned and looked at him then, because she wanted to see whether he knew what he had said. He did not. His face was grey and set and fixed on the wall of the Grey, and his jaw was clenched against the shaking, and there was nothing in it of the boy in the gardens at all.

She looked back at the fog.

"They do not want anything," she said. "That is the first thing, and the thing nobody believes. People want them to be hungry, or angry, or sorry. They are none of those things. They are bodies that have been given an instruction. Lucan's priests, or Livia, or whatever is in the Spire that taught them, take a dead thing and write a sentence into it, the way you write on a wax tablet, and it does the sentence until the wax is gone." She heard her own voice steady as she went, as it always steadied, the way a horse steadies on a road it knows. "The sentence is short. *Walk. Hold. Kill what lives.* They cannot read it themselves. They cannot argue with it. They do not tire, and they do not feel the cold. The cold *keeps* them. The cold is why the war goes as it does in winter."

"And the water."

"The water they cannot write on. That is the one mercy in it." She was quiet a moment. "Whatever drowns stays drowned. The deep keeps its own ledger, the sailors say, and for once the sailors are right. Not Lucan's priests, not Livia, not anyone has ever raised a man the sea took." She paused. "So they do the other thing. They raise the dead on land, and then they walk them into the water. Down the beach and under. In a ford. In a harbor mouth. They leave them standing on the bottom of the sea, in the dark, in the cold that keeps them, wherever they are useful, for as long as the sentence lasts. At Corvus they walked the dead down the slipways into the harbor and left them there for a week. The fishermen who came out after saw them at low water. In rows."

He did not say anything. His hands had stopped knocking. She looked and saw that he had closed them on the rail, hard, until the knuckles stood out.

"Steel slows them," she said. "Steel takes off the parts that hold the sword. It does not rub out the sentence. Fire does, mostly. Fire takes the tablet away." She drew her right hand out of her sleeve and turned it palm up between them, in the last of the light, and showed him the small white burn across the ball of her thumb. "Fire with a particular sign on it does it better. I found that at Hollowmere, in a ditch, on a man who had been a carter. I don't know why it works. I have three theories and no time. I know that it does."

He looked at the burn. Then at her face. Then, carefully, as if afraid of being caught, at the burn again.

"How many casks," he said, "to a man?"

She almost laughed. It was such a clerk's question. It was exactly her question; she had asked it of herself on the quay, and of the ditch at Hollowmere, and of the dark under the roof at the lodging-house with the candle out.

"If they are close together, and in water, and the oil can be made to spread, one cask may answer for a dozen. If they are scattered on dry ground, one cask is one man, and a poor bargain." She put her hand away. "Thirty-one casks. Your servant got them on the king's credit, I'm told, which I take to mean that we are all thieves now, and the cohort may as well be armed."

"Felix," Marcus said, almost to himself, "is going to be hanged before any of us."

"Felix will talk the hangman into apologizing."

That got something out of him, a short breath through the nose that in a better week might have been a laugh. It went away at once. He stood looking at the fog, and she stood looking at it, and the sea-smoke went past the rail in long, slow breaths, as if the island ahead of them were asleep and dreaming.

"Alana," he said.

She knew the tone. She had been waiting for it for three days without knowing she was waiting, the way one waits for a tooth to go. It was the voice of a man who has been rehearsing a thing in the dark, and has decided, against all good sense, to say it in the daylight.

She waited.

He did not say it.

She heard him draw breath for it, once, and let the breath go. She heard him draw it again. Out of the corner of her eye she saw his mouth open, and his jaw work, and his mouth close. His hands had begun knocking on the rail again, very softly, like someone at a door who is not sure he will be let in and is no longer sure he ought to be.

The silence went on long enough to become a thing in itself, a third person standing at the rail between them.

"Yes, Highness?" she said at last.

"Nothing." He looked down at his hands. "Mind the oil, in the fog. Keep a man on the casks."

"I always mind the oil."

"Yes," he said. "I've seen."

She turned from the rail. She went down the deck toward the hatch, briskly, the way she had walked out of the Lesser Hall, and did not count her steps, and did not look back.

*Note,* she thought, as the ladder took her down into the tar-dark of the stern. *Subject said please.*

*Subject began a sentence and did not finish it. Subject has done this, by my count, three times today.*

*Subject's hands stopped shaking while she spoke of the dead.*

*Not his. Hers.*


---

# Chapter Five: The Kettle Bank

**MARCUS**

By the first watch they were in the Grey, and the world had gone.

He had sailed in fog before. Every man of the isles had. He had stood at the rails of troopships and seen the sea smoke close around them like a fist and open again a mile on, and he had thought he knew what it was. He had not known. This was not a fog that lay on the water. It was a fog that *was* the water, that came up off it in slow, enormous, yellow-grey breaths and took the ship in and did not let it go. It stank of struck matches and rotten eggs and, under those, the hot wet mineral breath of Cyrene's spring, an island he could not see, exhaling somewhere ahead of them in the dark. The lantern at the stern made a ball of dirty gold about the length of a man's arm, and beyond the ball there was nothing. Not dark. Dark would have been honest. It was a soft, endless, faintly luminous nothing, the color of an old man's eye.

The cold had changed too. They had come out of the ice-sea into warmer water, and the air was not so bitter now, but it was wet. It got into the wool and the leather and the joints of a man's fingers, and it clung.

He was sweating anyway.

It had come on at noon, while he was handing out the swords, and it had not stopped. Cold sweat, rank, running down his ribs under his tunic, and with it a pain behind his eyes like a nail being turned, and his tongue thick, and his heart going too fast for no reason at all, running in his chest like a man running from something he cannot see. Three days. He knew this country. He had come to the edge of it before, in camps, when the wine ran out, and turned back. He had always turned back. There had always been a jar.

There was no jar on the *Clemency*. He had seen to that. He had had Felix go through the waist that afternoon and take every skin and flask and bottle the cohort had smuggled aboard, and pour them over the side, while two hundred men watched with the faces of mourners at a pyre. He had made himself stand and watch it too.

*You stupid, proud, self-murdering fool.*

He could hear the bells.

That was the worst of it. Not the sweat, not the shaking, not the nail. He could hear the bells of the Old Hill as clearly as if he stood on the quay, the great bronze throat of the Hall of Kings and the Fishmarket bell and the cracked bell of the Gaol Stair, ringing the hours for Titus across a hundred miles of sea, and he knew that they were not there. He knew it. He turned his head to listen and they came from the other side.

*Let them ring,* he told himself, and his own voice in his head was a stranger's.

He went over it again, at the rail, in the dark, because there was nothing else to do with his mind but that or the bells.

*I'm sorry about the sonnet.* That was the first one, and the simplest, and he had had it ready in his mouth. He had not said it, because he had heard how it would sound before it was out: a boy's apology for a boy's prank, small and neat as a coin, offered to a woman who had walked out of a burning city with a library's ashes in her hair. As though the sonnet were the thing. As though one line of sorry could be laid against eight years and come out even.

*You had a good ear for the meter. I remember thinking it was good, even while I was reading it in your voice.* That was true. He had only found it was true at the rail, with her talking about the dead in that dry, steady, lecturing voice, and the memory coming up out of the cellar of him whole: the paper in his hand, and the lines on it, and a small cold surprise somewhere under the wine that they were *good*, better than anything the court poets read at Midwinter, and that he was going to make a table full of drunk men laugh at them anyway. He had not said that either. It was worse than the first. It meant he had known.

*I didn't know it was you, in the violet. At the dance.* A lie. He had known. He remembered the color now. He remembered that she had walked the whole length of the Lesser Hall, and that the hall had watched her do it, and that he had felt the eyes of his friends on him and wanted to be the sort of man who did not need to be walked to. He had turned to the herald's daughter because it was easy. He could not now remember the herald's daughter's name.

*You're the bravest thing on this ship. Your voice doesn't shake when you talk about the dead, and you've a burn on your thumb from finding out, alone, in a ditch, how to kill a thing that won't stay killed, while I was in the Drowned Lantern finding out how much wine it takes to stop dreaming. I'd follow you into the Grey with a lantern. I'm going to.*

That one he had not even begun. It had stuck somewhere under his breastbone, behind the oilskin packet, and stayed there.

Every one of them, he understood, was for him. Every one was a thing he wanted to put down so that he could stop carrying it, and the only place to put it down was on her. She had carried enough out of Corvus. She did not need to carry his sorry too, on a prison hulk, on the night before the dead.

So he had said nothing. He had said *mind the oil*.

*Coward,* said a voice in his head that sounded like Titus.

*Maybe,* he answered it. *Or maybe for once in my life I held my tongue when it was the right thing to do, and I can't tell the difference anymore.*

The bells rang on, for Titus, from the wrong side of the ship.

---

"Four fathoms," said Nerva. "Black sand, and shell."

The captain knelt by the rail amidships with the lead-line coiled at his knee and the lead itself in his hand, a long dull plug of metal the size of a man's forearm. He had armed its hollow foot with tallow before he cast it, and now he turned it over in the lantern-light and looked at what the tallow had brought up from the bottom. He pressed a thumb into it. He smelled it. Then, to Marcus's faint disgust, he touched it with the tip of his tongue.

"Kettle Bank," he said. "South end of it. We're a mile and a half off the headland, maybe two, and we're set east." He looked up into the fog, not at anything, the way a blind man looks toward a voice. "I know the smell of it. Shipyards. Pitch and steamed oak. Can you smell it, Highness? Under the sulphur?"

Marcus breathed in. Rotten eggs. Wet wool. His own sweat, foul. And then, under them, faint and sad as the smell of a house where someone has died, the smoke. Burned pitch. Burned oak. Eleven days of it, cold now, soaked into the fog like a stain into linen.

"Yes," he said.

"They've stopped burning. That's something." Nerva coiled the line. "I want the oars in, Highness. All of them. I want to drift on the set and listen for the surf on the headland, and when I hear it I'll know where we are within a cable, and then we'll go in. But I want it quiet. No drum. No talking in the waist. If there's a man aboard who snores, I want him awake."

"Do it."

Nerva did it. The word went down the benches in whispers, and the oars came in, one by one, scraping in their ports, and were laid along the thwarts; and the drum-boy, Kaeso, who had beaten the stroke for three days until his hands bled through the rags, laid down his sticks by the mast and sat with his arms around his knees and his eyes very wide.

And the *Clemency* drifted.

There was no sound. That was what Marcus would remember after. Not the dark, or the stink, or the cold. The silence. Two hundred men on the benches and not one of them breathing above a sip. The ship's old timbers easing and creaking. Water talking very quietly along the strakes. The lantern's small hiss. Far off, or not far, there was no telling, a long, low, sighing roar that came and went, came and went, like the breathing of something very large asleep.

"Surf," breathed Nerva. "There. Hear it? On the bow. Off the port bow."

Marcus heard it. He thought he heard it. He stood beside the captain with his fists closed at his sides to keep them still, and listened, and the bells rang for Titus in his head, and the surf sighed, and something touched the hull.

It was very small. A knock. The sound a knuckle makes on a door, once, politely, under the waterline, below where he stood.

Nerva's head came round.

Another knock. Further aft. Then one forward, at the bows. Then two together, under the waist, where the cohort sat on their benches, and Marcus saw the men there hear it and look down at the planking under their feet.

"Ice," somebody whispered.

"There's no ice here," said Nerva. "Not in the warm water." He had gone very still. "Highness. The lead."

The lead-line lay coiled at his knee where he had left it, with the tallowed plug at the end of its tail on the deck. Except that it was not on the deck. As Marcus looked at it, the line was paying out over the rail. Slowly. Hand over hand, the way a man pays out a line when he is lowering something heavy and does not want to drop it. Coil after coil of wet hemp sliding up off the deck and over the rail and down into the dark water, with nobody touching it at all.

Then it stopped. Went taut. And pulled.

"Gods," said Pell, at the steering oar.

Marcus took the knife from his belt and cut the line.

It whipped down over the rail and was gone without a splash. And in the silence afterward, under the waist, under the bows, under the stern where Alana's oil sat in its casks against the bulkhead, the knocking began in earnest. Not a knuckle. Many. A hundred hands. Flat palms and fists and fingers on the old wet timbers of the *Clemency*, on every side of her at once, patient and unhurried and absolutely without feeling, like rain beginning on a roof.

"*Shields!*" Marcus roared, and his voice tore out of him, and all the silence broke at once. "Rufo! Shields to the rail! *Every man with iron, on your feet!*"

---

They came up through the oar-ports.

He had not thought of that. Nobody had. The *Clemency* had sixty ports, thirty a side, and half of them still boarded from her years as a hulk, and the other half open, and the oars laid inboard along the thwarts so the ports were bare black holes in the hull an arm's length above the water. And out of the holes, in the dirty gold of the lanterns, came the hands.

Grey hands. Bloated, wrinkled, white-nailed, the skin gone loose and soft as wet paper, groping in over the sills and finding the thwarts and the men's ankles and the oar-looms, and taking hold. Then the arms behind them. Then the heads.

Men screamed. Men fell back off the benches into the men behind them. A man on the port side, three benches forward, was taken by the ankle and dragged, howling, feet-first toward the port he had been rowing through for three days, and his friend caught him round the chest and held, and the two of them were drawn together across the wet planking like a cart with its wheels locked. The thing in the port had him by the leg. Marcus could see its face, framed in the oar-hole like a picture in a window. A woman. A woman of fifty, perhaps, in a fishwife's leather apron with the strings still tied. Her grey hair floated loose around her and her eyes were open and white and empty, and she pulled.

Marcus was there before he knew he had moved.

He did not remember the space between the rail and the bench. He was at the rail and then he was on the bench, and the gladius was in his hand, and he brought it down across the woman's wrists where they gripped the man's leg, once, twice, a butcher's stroke, and the hands came away. The man fell back into his friend's arms. The handless thing in the port did not scream. It did not seem to notice. It only reached in again with its two stumps, groping, still pulling at nothing.

He put the sword through its eye. It sank back out of the port and was gone.

And then he understood that his hands were not shaking.

He looked down at them in the half-dark. The right one on the hilt. The left one braced on the wet thwart. Steady. Steady as a carpenter's. The sweat was still running down his ribs and the nail was still in his skull and the bells were still ringing, somewhere, for Titus, but his hands had stopped as if somebody had laid a palm on them.

He did not stop to wonder at it. There was no time to wonder at anything.

"*Up! Get up!*" He was on his feet on the bench, bellowing down the length of the waist. "Off the benches! Away from the ports! Into the middle, all of you, *into the middle*! Rufo! Give me a wall!"

And the optio gave him one.

Marcus saw it come together out of the chaos the way he had once seen a legion form on a beach, in his first summer, and thought it the most beautiful thing in the world. Rufo was in the middle of the waist by the mast, with a shield on his arm and his voice going like a whip. *Here. You. Here. Shields up. Lock it. Lock it, you whoreson, are you holding a door or a dinner-plate.* And men were coming to him. Not all of them. Not even most. But one, and then three, and then a dozen, stumbling across the thwarts with the condemned shields of the Fourteenth on their arms and the faded red *XIV* catching the lantern-light, and Rufo was shoving them into a line on either side of the mast, facing out, a ring, a ragged ring of shields with the drum-boy and the old men and the swordless ones penned in the middle of it. And the drowned dead came up out of the oar-ports onto the benches, dripping, slow, many, and walked into the shields.

The sound of it was a sound Marcus knew. Wet weight on wood. A long grinding shove. Men grunting, bracing, their feet sliding on the slurry of the deck.

"*Hold!*" Rufo's voice. "*Hold, and stab low! Low! Under the rim! Take their legs!*"

They held.

---

Afterward Marcus could not have said how long it went on. It seemed an hour. It may have been less than the turning of a glass.

He remembered fragments. He remembered a shipwright, a big man in a leather jerkin with an adze still thrust through his belt, coming over the port rail at him with seaweed in his beard and his jaw hanging loose on one side, and how the adze-handle had knocked against the rail as he climbed, *clock, clock,* like a man knocking to be let in. He remembered hewing at the shipwright's knee and the knee giving, and the man going down and still crawling, and Roderic appearing out of the fog with a boathook as long as a pike and hooking the thing under the arm and heaving it, bodily, back over the side, as a man heaves a sack of grain off a cart.

"*That's one for the fish,*" the General said, and spat after it. He was breathing hard. His face was wet. Marcus did not think it was spray.

And he remembered the sound Iason made, behind him, when the shipwright went over the rail. It was not a scream. It was a name, said once, high and cracked, the way a boy calls across a yard to a man he has worked beside every day for years: *Master Philon!* And then the young man of Cyrene was at the rail with his sword hanging from his hand, looking down into the water where the adze-handle had gone under, and Marcus took him by the collar and hauled him back into the line, and Iason let himself be hauled, and did not say anything else at all.

He remembered Oppius the baker standing at the end of Rufo's line with no shield at all, his sword lost or never drawn, holding a drowned boy off the drum-boy with his two bare hands around its throat, and weeping, and not letting go.

He remembered the cooper, Calvo. *Eleven crowns to the Silk Row, Highness, if you're keeping a ledger.* Calvo had been on the port side, at the end of the shield-wall, with his spear, and a thing had come up out of the port behind him while he faced front, and taken him around the waist from behind like a lover, and simply gone back out of the port again, backwards, the way it had come, without hurry, taking him with it. Calvo had not screamed. There had been a sound, very short, as he struck the sill. Then a splash. Then nothing. The spear lay on the bench where he had stood.

Marcus had been six paces away. He had seen it the whole way. He had not been able to cross the six paces.

*Septimus Calvo.*

He remembered Felix. That was the strangest of all of it, afterward. Felix with a lantern in one hand and a cooking-cleaver in the other, standing over the hatch to the stern cabin, quite white, quite calm, saying, to a drowned thing coming at him over the bench, in exactly the tone he used to tell Marcus that the tailor was at the door: *I'm sorry. This part of the ship is not available.* And bringing the cleaver down.

And he remembered the light.

A crack, flat and enormous, like a mast splitting in a gale, and a white bolt coming down the length of the waist from the stern. It jumped from one grey head to the next to the next, three of them, four, in the space of a blink, and they went down smoking, and the smell of it came after, hot iron and burned hair and the sharp clean reek of a summer storm. He turned with every other man on the ship, half-blind, and saw her on the afterdeck above the hatch, with a rod of plain glass in her hand, and the glass still burning white along its whole length like a bar drawn from a forge.

---

**ALANA**

She came up the ladder with the rod already in her hand.

She had felt the knocking through the hull before anyone on deck had heard it. She had been in the stern cabin with the lamp turned low and the oil-casks around her, and her palm flat on the lid of the trunk, as it was every night, and the trunk had, under her hand, very slightly, *turned*. Not moved. Turned. As a sleeper turns toward a sound. And then she had heard the first knock, under the planking, under her feet, and she had known what it was before the lead-line went over the rail, and she had been on the ladder before the prince began to shout.

The servant, Felix, was standing over the hatch with a bloody cleaver in his hand, and he stepped aside for her as if he were holding a door.

"Lady—"

"Not a lady. Stay there. Nothing comes down this hatch."

She stood on the afterdeck and looked down into the waist, and for one heartbeat, despite everything, the scholar in her simply *looked*.

The prince's wall was a ring around the mainmast, perhaps forty shields, ragged, bowed in the middle where the press was heaviest, but holding. The dead were all around it. They had come up out of the oar-ports on both sides, onto the benches and the thwarts, and they filled the waist like water filling a hold, wet and grey and patient, pressing in on the shields with their swollen hands. She saw a man in a rope-maker's apron. A woman with a child's shawl around her neck, and no child. A boy of perhaps twelve. She saw the knots of blue cord on two of them, at the wrist, that the fishwives of the southern isles tied on for luck, and she understood, with the cold clarity that came on her at the worst times, who these were.

*Not drowned,* she thought. *The men will call them the drowned, and they are not. The sea keeps what it takes, and nobody, not Lucan, not Livia, has ever made it give one back. These died ashore. Eleven days ago, when the fort fell. In the lanes and on the slips and in their own doorways. And Lucan's people raised them where they lay, and walked them down the beach into the cold, and out along the bottom onto the bank, and left them standing there in the dark to keep the door.*

*They are not Tharsians. They are Cyrene.*

She wanted, very badly, for one moment, to be sick. She set it aside. It could be done later. Everything could be done later.

She raised the rod.

The rune for fire was the first one any student of Corvus learned, and the last one the masters let them use: a hook and a cross-stroke and a closing turn, drawn on the air with the tip of the glass. She drew it on a knot of three dead pressing at the port end of the ring, and spoke its name, and the glass went hot in her fist, and the air in front of the rod folded in on itself like a page burning from the middle. A fist of fire went down the waist and burst among the three of them. They went up. They did not scream. They had nothing to scream with. They burned, very upright, and bent, and fell across the benches, and the shields closed over the gap where they had been.

*Three.*

She drew it again. Two more, at the starboard rail, climbing. The rod was so hot now that she could smell her own palm. She changed her grip and drew the other one, the one she liked less, the long jagged stroke and the two short barbs that the old books called the *spear of the sky*. It tore out of the glass with a crack that shook the deck under her feet, white, branching, and leapt from a grey head to a grey shoulder to a grey head, four of them at the starboard ports, and dropped them all, smoking, back into the black water they had come out of.

*Nine.*

Her nose was bleeding. She felt it run warm over her lip and did not wipe it. Fire. *Ten. Eleven.* The sky's spear again, and the crack of it, and the cohort flinching under their shields and then, some of them, a ragged cheer. *Fifteen.* She had never done so many so fast. At Hollowmere it had been one carter in a ditch, and she had sat down afterward in the mud and shaken for an hour.

*Seventeen.*

And then she looked over the stern rail, down into the water, where the lantern-light went a little way into the black.

They were standing on the bank.

A fathom down. Two. Beyond the reach of the light and then, as her eyes learned the dark, within it. Grey faces turned up toward the ship like flowers toward a sun, rank after rank of them, shoulder to shoulder on the black sand, on every side of the hull, as far as the light went and, she had no doubt at all, much farther. And they were climbing. Slowly, without hurry, the way they did everything, hand over hand up the weeded strakes of the *Clemency* toward the open ports. For every one she had burned, there were twenty in the water. There were not dozens. There were hundreds.

*Arithmetic,* she thought, very clearly. *The rod is one hand and one rune at a time. Seventeen in, I am bleeding and my hand is cooked. The rod will not answer this. The rod is a knife, and this is a harvest.*

She turned and dropped down the ladder past Felix.

"Get two men," she said over her shoulder. "The big one, the baker. And the drum-boy. Tell them to bring the casks up as fast as they can carry them, and not to drop one, and not to bring a light within a pace of them. *Go.*"

He went.

The first cask was too heavy for her and she did not care. She knocked the lashings off it in the dark of the stern cabin, and got it onto her shoulder, and came up the ladder with it against her neck, and the rod thrust through her belt still smoking.

"Marcus!"

He was at the port rail, fighting. She had not known he could fight like that. She had seen him over the table in the Lesser Hall, all fury and no art, a bull through a fence. This was something else. He had a short sword and a boathook-shaft in his two hands and he was holding the gap between the end of the shield-ring and the rail on his own, and he moved in the gap like a man doing something he had done many times and hated every time, without a wasted step, without a sound. The thing in front of him was a shipwright with an adze in its belt. He took its knee. He took its hand. It came on. He took the other knee.

"*Marcus!*"

He heard her. He looked up, across the whole length of the waist, through the fog and the bodies and the gold of the lanterns, and found her on the afterdeck with the cask against her neck.

She did not have to explain. She saw him see the cask. She saw him understand. She would think about that afterward, for a long time, with the weather-book open and the pen dry: that she had not had to say anything at all.

"*Rufo!*" the prince roared. "*Shields up and close! Close it! Get them off the benches and into the middle! Everyone into the middle! Away from the rail!*"

And the ring tightened.

She knocked the bung out of the cask with the cooper's mallet, and tipped it, and poured.

---

The oil went down the afterdeck ladder in a thick gold rope and spread across the planking of the waist, and ran, the way oil runs, into every low place: under the benches, along the thwarts, out through the scuppers and the open oar-ports and down the old black sides of the *Clemency* into the sea. The drowned walked through it. They did not care. It was only wet. Wet on wet.

The baker came up the hatch with a cask in each arm, and the drum-boy came up behind him with one, staggering, and she took them and broke them and poured them, one, and two, and three, over the rail of the afterdeck and down the sides of the ship to starboard, and to port, and Felix came with a fifth. The sea took the oil as the deck had. A sheen spread out from the hull over the black water, slow and wide and shining in the lantern-light like a skin of beaten gold. Things moved under it. Many things. Standing on the bank in the dark, a fathom down, two, with their faces turned up toward the light.

Seven casks. Eight. Her shoulders were screaming. The oil was in her hair and on her robes, on the Corvus silk, the academy's last bolt. She did not care.

Then she knelt on the wet planking of the afterdeck, and dipped her right forefinger in the oil, and drew the sign.

She had never drawn it so large. At Hollowmere it had been the size of her palm, scratched on a dead carter's breastplate with a knife-point. Now she drew it the width of the deck, in oil, with her finger and then with the flat of her hand, the long first stroke and the turning stroke and the three short cuts across, and she was speaking under her breath as she did it, the words that went with it, which were not words exactly, which were the names of the strokes in the old high script that no one had used for a thousand years but the masters of Corvus, and now not even them. It took perhaps the space of ten breaths. In the waist below, men were dying while she drew. She knew it. She drew it right.

When it was done she stood up, and took the lantern off its hook at the stern rail, and looked down into the waist.

"*Marcus!*" she called. "*Your men!*"

"*Down!*" he bellowed. "*Every man down! On your faces! Now!*"

And the cohort went down. All of them. Rufo's ring folded inward and dropped behind the shields like a fist closing, and the drowned dead stood over them for a moment, swaying, in the gold-sheened waist, with nothing to push against.

She dropped the lantern onto the sign.

---

It did not burn the way oil burns. She had known it would not. She was still not ready for it.

Ordinary oil, lit, makes a soft, sullen, smoky fire, a kitchen fire, that crawls. This went out from the sign like a word spoken in a quiet room. It ran. It ran across the afterdeck and down the ladder and across the waist in a single bright breath, white at its heart and gold at its edge, and it took every drowned thing it touched as dry thatch takes a spark. They went up like torches. They did not scream. They had nothing to scream with. They only stood, burning, very upright, and then they bent, slowly, the way a tallow candle bends in a hot room, and sank onto the planking, and were gone.

The prince's men lay on their faces under their shields with the fire running over the deck around them, and it did not touch them. It ran around them, the way a stream runs around a stone. She had not known it would do that. She had hoped. She had three theories and no time.

And then the fire went over the side.

It went down the black flanks of the *Clemency* and out through the oar-ports and down into the gold skin of oil on the sea, and the sea caught. All around them, in a great ring, a cable wide, the water went up in light. And the fire did not stop at the surface. That was the thing she would see for the rest of her life, whenever she closed her eyes. It went *down*. Down through the black water, in long, slow, shining threads, like roots, like lamplight seen through a cellar floor, seeking. And wherever it found something standing on the bank down there in the dark, it took hold.

Under the sea, one after another, the drowned of Cyrene began to burn.

They were lights. That was all she could see of them, from the afterdeck. A fathom down, two fathoms, standing on the Kettle Bank in their hundreds in the dark: small, steady, golden lights, like lanterns, like the lamps of a town seen from the sea at night. A whole drowned town of lanterns, burning under the water, lighting up the fog from below so that the whole of the Grey for a mile around them turned to gold, and then, one by one, going out.

*The Drowned Lantern,* she thought, absurdly. *That was the name of his tavern.*

She found she was weeping. She noted it, distantly, as she noted the burn on her right hand, where the lantern-handle had been, and the oil burning in the hem of her robe, which she put out by kneeling on it.

"Gods," said a voice beside her, very quietly. It was the captain. Nerva stood at the stern rail with Pell beside him at the steering oar, and the gold light under the sea was on both their faces, and on Nerva's it made him look very old. "Oh, gods. I've sailed with men from this town."

He was quiet a long moment. Then he said, without turning: "They'll have seen that from the headland, lady. They'll have seen that from the moon."

"I know," said Alana.

---

**MARCUS**

The light under the water went out a little before dawn. The fog stayed gold for some time after that, and then, slowly, it was only grey again.

They counted at first light.

He did it himself, walking the waist with Rufo a pace behind him, the way he had walked the column on the quay with Roderic a pace behind; and the ship was quiet, and the men sat on the benches in their blackened cloaks with their eyes on him as he passed, and nobody said anything. The deck was scorched in long pale streaks, as though a giant had dragged a hot iron across it. In the low places, under the thwarts, there was a fine grey ash. It was very soft. He did not look at it closely.

Calvo, taken through the port. Fabius, the old debtor who had wept when they gave him a sword: they found him under the bench where he had crawled, with no mark on him at all. His heart, Rufo thought. A man named Tullus, a horse-thief, who had been on the starboard side when the first ports went. And one man whose name nobody knew. He had come aboard in the third ten, and he had not spoken in three days, and he had not come to the chest for a sword, and none of the men who had been chained beside him for a month in the dark knew what he was called. They had called him the Vessan, because of his accent. That was all. He had gone over the side somewhere in the dark, and no one had seen it happen.

Hostus the barber was still at work by the mast when the count was done, on his knees in the ash with his sleeves rolled and his curved needle flashing, stitching a bitten forearm with the same small, patient, disapproving stitches he had used, he said, on a hundred drunk scalps in Tanner's Lane. Oppius had the galley-fire lit and was boiling the green pork in seawater, because it would be eaten whether it was fit or not, and he meant it to be eaten hot. Iason sat alone on the starboard bench with his sword across his knees and looked at nothing.

Four. Two hundred and eight.

And the drowned of Cyrene, whom nobody would count at all.

Marcus stood by the mainmast when it was done, and found that his hands had started again. A small tremor, a plucked string. It had come back sometime after the fire went out. The bells had stopped, though. He did not know when. He listened for them and there was only the sea, and the creak of the old ship, and far off, on the port bow, the long low sigh of surf on rock.

He took the oilskin packet out of his tunic.

It was warm from his body. He unwrapped it with his shaking fingers, in the grey light, and took out the single sheet inside, folded in four, soft as cloth from the folding, and opened it. Ninety-one names in his own hand. Three columns. The ink gone brown. *Tiberius* at the top of the first column, because he had been first. And then the rest of them, every one, in the order they had stood on the stair.

He looked at the bottom of the third column, where there was room.

*No new names.*

He had promised it to a sheet of paper. It was the only promise he had kept in three years.

"You'll want this."

Alana was beside him. He had not heard her come. She had a bandage on her right hand, a rag tied clumsily with her teeth, and the hem of her robe was charred black to the knee, and there was oil in her hair, and her eyes were red. She was holding out her weather-book, open, and the pen.

He looked at it. The open page was covered in her neat small hand: *Wind S by E, light. Fog dense, yellowing at the foot. Lead 4 fms, black sand & shell (Kettle Bank, per Capt.).* And then, below that, in the same hand, not quite as neat: *Drowned of Cyrene. Several hundred.* And nothing else.

"Not on that," she said, and nodded at the old sheet in his hands. "I know what that is. Your servant told me. Not on that." She turned the book over in her hands and opened it from the back, to a clean page, and held it out again. "On this. Separately. A different roll."

He stood very still.

"Why?" he said.

"Because you will not remember them otherwise. You will try. You will remember the cooper, because of the eleven crowns, and you will remember the old man because he wept. You will not remember the horse-thief, and nobody remembers the Vessan already. Not even the men who slept beside him." She did not look away from him. "And because you said you would, Highness. On the deck, yesterday, handing out the swords. *So that if you die on this ship I'll know what to call you.* I was there. I heard it. I have it written down."

He took the pen. His hand shook. He wrote anyway, on the clean back page of her weather-book, a hard bad hand that blotted.

*Septimus Calvo, cooper. Fabius, debtor. Tullus, of the horses. The Vessan.*

He looked at it. It was not enough. It would never be enough.

"It's a start," Alana said. "That is all a roll ever is."

She took back the book and the pen, and closed the book, and put it inside her cloak against her ribs. And she did not leave. She stood beside him at the mast, a hand's breadth off, and looked forward with him into the thinning grey.

He felt her there the way he had felt the fire go out in the night, all at once, by the warmth. There was ash on her cheek. She had not noticed it. Her bandaged hand was held a little away from her body, the way people hold a hurt they mean to ignore, and her eyes were red, and she was looking at Cyrene's fog with the same patient, measuring attention she gave to casks and weather and the dead, as if it were one more sum that would come out if she only worked it long enough.

He thought of telling her that she had saved the ship. She knew. He thought of telling her that he had seen the dead burn under the sea, the whole drowned town of them going out one by one like lamps at the end of a feast, and that it was the most terrible and the most beautiful thing he had ever seen, and that she had made it. She knew that too. He thought, for one long, foolish heartbeat, of reaching over and wiping the ash off her cheek with his thumb.

He did nothing. He stood beside her and said nothing, and let her stand there, and it seemed to him the only gift he had that she might be willing to take.

---

The fog was lifting.

It went the way the Grey went near an island in the first of the morning, Nerva had told him: not all at once, but in rents and tatters, torn open by the land-wind coming down off the hills, so that pieces of the world appeared and vanished like things glimpsed through a moving crowd. A stretch of black water, glassy, with a single gull riding it. A line of white where the swell broke on a reef. And then, all at once, as if a curtain had been dragged aside, the headland.

It stood up out of the sea a mile off the port bow, black and sheer, a broken tooth of basalt against a sky only a little less black than itself. At its crown there was a fort, a squat dark shape of walls and one square tower, and on the tower a light was burning. Below it, along the curve of the bay to the east, there was a town, or the bones of one: roofs, and gaps in the roofs, and the long black ribs of the slipways running down into the water, charred, with the steam of Cyrene's spring rising white and slow among them as if the whole shore were quietly smoking still.

Marcus heard Roderic come up on his other side. He heard Felix. He heard the cohort rising from the benches behind him, all two hundred and eight of them, and coming to the rail to see.

Nobody spoke.

On the headland, high up, small and clear across the water, a bell began to ring.

It was not ringing for Titus. It was ringing for them.

"Cyrene, Highness," said Nerva quietly, from the steering oar. "As promised."

Marcus turned his back on it.

He climbed onto the lid of the empty arms chest by the mast, because it was the only high place in the waist, and stood there above them in the grey light with his scorched cloak hanging off him and his hands shaking at his sides. Two hundred and eight faces looked up at him. Black with smoke. Grey with sickness and no sleep. Rufo's, set. Iason's, empty. Oppius's one eye, wet. The drum-boy, Kaeso, with his bloody rag-wrapped sticks in his lap.

"Look behind you," he said.

His voice came out a croak. Nobody moved. He said it again, and this time it went down the waist like a stone thrown down a well. "*Look behind you.*"

They turned. Two hundred and eight heads, and behind them the Grey, standing up off the sea in a yellow wall without a door in it.

"That's the way home," Marcus said. "There's a gallows at the end of it. And a cell. And a magistrate who has already crossed out your name." He held up his closed fist. "My father gave us one stone for this voyage. It points that way. It's the only gift he sent. He thinks we'll need nothing else, because he thinks we're not coming back."

He let his hand fall.

"I'll tell you what you are. You know it already, but I'll say it, because nobody else ever will to your faces. You're what the capital swept out of its corners. You're the men they send so that better men don't have to go. Fodder. Nobody's waiting on the quay for you. Nobody's lit a lamp. Your wives stopped coming to the prison gate a long time ago. Your children have stopped asking. And if you die on that beach this morning, there's not a clerk in Aeridor who'll trouble to write it down."

The bell rang. Nobody looked at it.

"I'm the same." It came out of him rough, and he let it. "My brother's in the hill with his hair combed the way he hated. Ninety-one of my men are on a stair I was carried off asleep. My father put me on this ship because he couldn't bear to look at me. There's not a soul on this deck who wasn't thrown away. Not one. Not me."

He looked down at them. At Rufo's hard face. At Oppius, with his one wet eye. At Iason, who had not lifted his head since the night.

"So hear me. A thing that's been thrown away belongs to nobody. Not to the magistrate. Not to the turnkey. Not to the king. This morning, maybe for the first time in your lives, you belong to *yourselves*. And a man who belongs to himself chooses what he dies for."

Something moved in the waist. Not a sound. A shifting, the way a field shifts when the wind comes into it.

"Last night the dead came up out of the dark at us. And you *stood*. Thieves and debtors and deserters, in a ring around a drum-boy, in the dark, with nobody watching, and you did not break. I have seen the legions break for less. Calvo didn't run. Fabius didn't drop his sword. You are not what they said you were. You proved it last night, and nobody saw it but each other."

His hands were shaking. He did not hide them.

"Every man here has lost someone. A wife. A son. A brother at Hollowmere. A town." He did not look at Iason, and every man on the ship knew who he meant. "They're in the hill, and in the fords, and in the water we came through last night. We can't bring one of them back. Not one. But we can go up that beach with their names in our mouths, and make the men who did it *hear* them.

"And maybe it isn't death up there. Maybe on the far side of that wall there's a morning when nobody puts you in irons. When you sleep under a roof you took with your own hands, and eat bread you earned, and when a man asks your name, you say it out loud, and it's a free man's name." His voice cracked. He let it. "I want that morning. I want it for every one of you. I haven't wanted anything in three years but the bottom of a jar, and I want that.

"There's no road back. The sea is behind us, and the rope behind the sea. There is only one way left, and it's forward. Up that beach. Over that wall. To the fort, or to the dark. If it's the dark, we meet it on our feet, facing it, and it'll have to come through our shields to take us.

"*Forward.* There's nothing else. Forward."

For a long moment there was only the bell.

Then Decimus Rufo, in the front of the waist, lifted the shield on his arm, the condemned shield of the Fourteenth with the faded red *XIV* by the boss, and struck the flat of his sword against it. Once. A dull flat crack, like a stone splitting.

The man beside him did it. Then the man beside him.

Somebody in the waist said it back to him. Hoarse, low, hardly more than a breath. *Forward.* Then ten of them. Then the whole ship, two hundred and eight thrown-away men saying it with their teeth shut, on the stroke of their shields, *forward, forward, forward*, like a ram knocking at a gate.

Then the drum-boy picked up his sticks.

It went down the waist like fire down an oil-slick, shield and spear-butt and sword-flat and fist on bench, until the whole of the old hulk was ringing with it, two hundred and eight men beating out a stroke that this time, every one of them kept; and over it, out across the water toward the headland and its small brave bell, the drum.

Marcus stood on the chest in the noise of it. In his cloak the King's Shard stirred, all by itself, the way a sleeping dog turns in its basket, and pointed away past the men and the stern and the Grey, toward home.

He turned his back on it, and faced the headland.

"Oars out," he said.


---

# Chapter Six: The Black Beach

**RODERIC**

His back had begun to talk to him somewhere in the night, and by the time the oars went out it would not shut up.

It was the old wound, low on the left, where a Tharsian halberd had found the gap under his plate on the Long Field nineteen years ago and a surgeon with a cold needle and a warm flask had sewn the gap closed again. It was a fine wound for a song. It was a poor wound for a man of fifty-two who had spent the night heaving drowned shipwrights over a rail with a boathook. The wet got into it. The wet got into everything in this damned fog. He stood by the mainmast with his weight on his right leg and his hand pressed flat over the place, the way a man holds a door shut against a wind, and watched the boy.

*Not a boy,* he told himself. *Not after that.*

He had heard a great many speeches before battles. He had made more than he could count, and some of them had been very good, and two or three of them had been sung afterward in the wine-halls of the capital by men who had not been there. He had heard Tiberius speak to the First Legion on the eve of the stair, in a voice like a sword being drawn slowly, and seen hardened men weep. He had never heard anything like what the boy had just said from the lid of a ration chest to two hundred thieves. There had been nothing in it about glory. Nothing about the king, or the isles, or the songs. The boy had stood up in front of the worst men in Aeridor with his hands shaking and told them they were rubbish, and that he was rubbish too, and that rubbish belonged to nobody, and they had *roared*.

Roderic did not understand it. He knew only that it had worked, and that he could not have done it, and that somewhere under his breastbone, behind the sore old pride of his house, something had gone very quiet and very proud at once.

*He'll ask me soon,* he thought. *Now he's sober and his voice works. He'll ask me where I was.*

The drum went on. The *Clemency* came about, heavy as a cow in mud, and put her old blunt bows toward the headland.

---

"I'll not take her into the harbor," said Nerva.

The captain stood at the steering oar beside Pell with the morning on his face, and the light was cruel to him. It showed every year. It showed the prison in him. But his voice had found its iron again, the hammer that came into it on deck, and he did not take his eyes off the shore.

"Look there, Highness. And there."

Marcus looked. Roderic looked with him.

The fog was going fast now, shredding off the water in long tails, and the bay of Cyrene lay open before them like a hand. The black headland and its fort on the right. The long curve of the shore running east, and the town crowded down along it, roofs and smoke-holes and the lanes between them climbing the hill. Below the town, the slipways. Even burned, they were a thing to see: four great ramps of dressed stone running down into the sea, wide enough for a trireme each, with the black skeletons of half-built hulls still standing on two of them and the white steam of the island's spring breathing up between their ribs. Across the mouth of the harbor, a mole of piled stone ran out from either shore toward the middle, leaving a gap a cable wide for ships to pass. On the end of each mole stood a squat round tower. There were two more on the waterfront, one at either end of the town.

Four towers. On every one, a red banner, hanging limp in the wet.

"The ports," Nerva said. "That's what the town calls them. Customs posts, when the yards paid customs. Each of those has a bolt-thrower on the roof that'll put a shaft through a ship's side at three hundred paces, and the two on the moles cross their shot right over the gap. That's why the Tharsians wanted the place. Whoever holds those four towers holds the bay." He spat over the rail. "I take her through that gap with the whole garrison awake, and they'll pin us to the water like a moth to a board."

"Then where?" said Marcus.

Nerva pointed with his chin. East, past the slipways, past the last houses of the town, where the shore ran out flat and black under a low line of dunes.

"The beach. Black sand. Steep, and soft, and no rock in it for a mile; I've careened ships there. I'll put her straight up it at full stroke." He paused. "She'll not come off again, Highness. Not without a hundred men and a spring tide and a better ship to pull her. You understand me? Once she's up, she's up."

Roderic watched the boy take that. It was the end of the last road home. The Grey behind them, and the King's Shard pointing back through it, and now no hull to carry them even if they wanted to go.

"Good," said Marcus.

Nerva looked at him sideways.

"She was a prison," Marcus said. "Let her end on a beach with her irons off. Do it, Captain."

And for the first time since Roderic had known him, Aulus Nerva smiled. It was not much. It was a crack in old wood. But it was there.

"*Ramming stroke!*" he bellowed, and the iron came into his voice like a bell. "*Kaeso! Give me ramming stroke and keep it till I say! Pull, you whoresons, pull like there's a magistrate behind you, because there is!*"

---

The tower at the east end of the waterfront saw them first.

Roderic watched the men on its roof run to the bolt-thrower, small black shapes against the white steam, and swing it round, and the long arm of it come up. He had time to think, very clearly, *They're good,* the way he would have thought it of any crew that laid a weapon that fast; and then the first bolt came.

He heard it before he saw it, a deep flat thrumming like a great bowstring loosed in an empty hall. It came over the water in a low arc, a shaft as long as a man with an iron head the size of a hand, and it went through the *Clemency*'s port bulwark forward of the mast as if the old oak were bread. It went through the bench behind the bulwark. It went through the rower on the bench. It went on out through the starboard side and into the sea, and the rower sat where he was, quite upright, with his hands still on the oar-loom and a hole in him the size of a fist, and then he slid sideways off the bench.

The men on either side of him did not stop rowing. Roderic would remember that.

"*Keep the stroke!*" roared Nerva. "*Keep it!*"

"Who was it?" said Marcus. He had not raised his voice. He did not take his eyes off the tower.

Rufo, at the mast, looked down the waist. "Bassus, Centurion. Forger."

"Lucius Bassus," said Marcus. "He did the warden's seal on his own release papers, and they hanged his brother for it instead." He said it the way a man reads a line off a page. "I've got him."

The second bolt went high. The third struck the water a spear's length off the bows and threw up a sheet of spray that came down over the rowers like a slap, and the drum did not falter, and the *Clemency* ran on. They were inside the range of the mole towers now, and the bolt-throwers on the moles swung round too, but they were too far, and they had been laid for the harbor gap, and their shafts fell short and wide in white splashes on the grey water.

Behind him, on the afterdeck, Roderic heard the crack.

He turned in time to see the white line of it leap out from the stern of the *Clemency* toward the east tower, a bolt of the sky's own fire, branching, enormous; and fall short. It burst against the water a hundred paces off the tower's foot in a great hiss of steam, and the sea there boiled for a heartbeat and was still. On the afterdeck the witch lowered her glass rod. She had her burned hand wrapped round it in its filthy rag, and she was white as a sail, and there was fresh blood on her upper lip.

"Too far," she said, to no one, and Roderic heard her through all the noise of the drum. "I'm sorry. It's too far. The air eats it."

"Save it," Roderic called to her. "Save it, lady, for the beach. You'll have work enough there."

She looked at him. Then she nodded, once, and sat down very suddenly on the deck with her back to the rail.

Roderic turned back to the bows. The beach came at them.

---

He had been in a ship that struck a rock once. This was not like that. A rock was a fist. This was a bed, a great black soft bed of sand rising out of the sea at a steep slant, and the *Clemency* ran up onto it at full ramming stroke with two hundred men at the oars, and the whole old hulk of her simply *stopped*, the way a running man stops when he runs into a wall of water.

Everything that was not tied down went forward. Men went off the benches in heaps. Oars snapped like kindling. The mast, the one mast they had given her back on the night before she sailed, whipped forward so hard Roderic heard its stays sing and one of them part with a crack like a whip, and the drum-boy went sliding the length of the waist on his backside with his drum in his arms, still holding it, still holding it. Roderic himself went down on one knee with a jolt that went up his spine into the old wound like a red-hot nail, and for a moment he saw nothing at all but a white flash, and heard nothing but his own breath.

Then the ship was still, and canted, and groaning in every timber, and he could hear the surf breaking against her stern, and the bell. The fort's bell. It had been ringing all this time.

"*Over the bows!*" That was Marcus. He was already up, already at the bow with his sword drawn, and the boy's voice went through the confusion in the waist like a spear through a wineskin. "*Over the bows and form on the sand! Rufo! Shields first! Atticus, your slingers to the left! Move! The sea's behind you, there's nowhere to stand but the beach! Move!*"

They went over the bows.

Roderic went with the second rank, because the first rank was Rufo's and he would not take it from the man, and because his back would not let him go any faster. He dropped off the bow-rail into the sea and the sea took him to the waist, and it was *warm*. He had not expected that. He had forgotten. It was the island's spring, running down out of the hills under the black sand and into the bay, and the surf was warm as a bath and stank of rotten eggs, and steam came off it in the cold air around the men's legs in long white curls so that the whole cohort seemed to wade ashore out of a kettle. His boots filled. His mail dragged at him. He waded, and the sand sucked at his feet under the water, and the surf shoved him from behind like an impatient friend, and then the water was at his knees, and then at his shins, and then he was out, on the black sand, which was hot underfoot even through his boots, and Rufo's line was forming in front of him, and the dead were coming down the beach.

---

Not many. That was the first thing he saw, and the thing that made him want to laugh aloud.

Not hundreds. Not the grey ranks that had stood on the Kettle Bank. Thirty, perhaps. Forty. They came over the low line of dunes at the head of the beach, out of the lanes at the eastern end of the town, slow and silent and stumbling in the soft sand: men and women of Cyrene in their aprons and their nightshirts, as the dead had been on the bank, but fewer. Behind them, on the dunes, Roderic saw what he was looking for. Living men. A file of soldiers in red cloaks and iron caps with crossbows, kneeling to span them, and an officer on a horse with his arm raised. And standing apart from them, on the crest of the highest dune, a single figure in a long dark coat with no weapon at all, only something white in its hand that might have been a book, or a tablet, or a sheaf of papers.

*That's the one,* thought Roderic. *That's the one doing the writing.*

He looked at the dead coming down the sand, and he thought of the drowned town of lights burning under the sea in the night, and he understood, and the laugh came up out of him after all, short and savage.
