"*They spent them!*" he shouted. "*Centurion! They spent them on the bank!*" Marcus turned, and Roderic saw that the boy had seen it too. "That's the whole of their dead, boy, *that's all they've got left*! They put the rest out to stand watch, and the witch burned the watch! *That's all they've got!*"

The crossbows loosed.

It was a ragged volley, fifteen bows, sixteen, and the bolts came down the beach thick and black and hit the shields of the Fourteenth with a sound like a fist of gravel thrown against a door. A man screamed in the second rank. Another went down with a bolt through his foot, pinned to the sand. Rufo's line flinched and held.

"*Atticus!*" Marcus roared.

On the left of the line, down by the water, the Vessan slinger and his eleven men were already whirling. Roderic had laughed at them, on the ship. Twelve thieves and poachers with bits of leather and cord, and a sack of beach stones. He did not laugh now. The slings went round once, twice, a low ugly buzzing like a hive kicked over, and then the stones went up the beach in a flat hard line, and on the dunes a crossbowman sat down hard with his face gone red and another dropped his bow and clutched his arm and the officer's horse reared and screamed. They whirled again. On the crest of the dune the crossbowmen ducked.

Then the sky cracked open behind him.

Roderic felt it in his teeth before he heard it, a flat enormous snap like a mast going in a gale, and a white bolt went over the heads of the line so close that the hair stood up on his arms under his mail. It struck the crest of the dune where the crossbowmen knelt, and branched, and leapt from one iron cap to the next to the next; and three men who had been spanning their bows were not kneeling anymore. They were lying in the sand with smoke coming off them, and the air stank of burned hair and hot iron and summer storms.

He turned his head. The witch was in the surf behind the line, up to her knees in the steaming water, with her robes floating out round her and the glass rod raised in her bandaged fist, white along its whole length. Her face was set like a schoolmistress's over a bad sum. She drew the long jagged stroke again on the air, and spoke, and the crack came again, and two more of them on the dune went over backward with their bows still in their hands.

*Save it for the beach,* he had told her. He had meant it kindly. He had not quite meant this.

And the dead came into the shields.

Rufo's line took them the way it had taken them on the deck. The long grinding shove. Stab low. Under the rim. These were fewer, and the line was wider now, on solid ground with the whole beach to spread on, and the men of the cohort knew what they were doing this time. It was ugly work, butcher's work, and it was done in the space of a dozen breaths. Roderic stood in the second rank and put the point of his sword through a dead fishwife's knee over a cohort man's shoulder and felt nothing at all about it, which was, he supposed, what war came to in the end.

Then he heard the hooves.

---

He knew them before he saw them. Fifty years of his life had been the sound of hooves, and he knew the size of the beast and the weight on it and the pace from the sound alone. Four riders. Heavy horse, or near it. Coming at a canter along the coast road behind the dunes, from the east, from the far end of the beach, where the dunes ran down low and the road came out onto the sand.

*Onto our flank,* he thought. *Onto the right of the line, where there's nobody but the swordless men and the boy.*

He looked to the right. The swordless men were there, the last dozen of the cohort who had come over the bows with boathooks and oars and bare hands. Kaeso was there, with his drum. Hostus the barber. Felix, with his cleaver. And the boy, Marcus, with his back to the dunes and his face to the line, shouting at Rufo, not hearing the hooves at all.

They came round the end of the dunes at a canter and broke into a gallop on the hard sand at the water's edge. Four Tharsian riders in scale and red cloaks, with lances down. Roderic saw the lead rider's face under his iron cap: a young man, with a short dark beard, grinning. He had picked his mark. He was riding straight for the man with the red crest.

Roderic did not decide anything. He was running before there was anything to decide.

He ran the way he had not run in ten years, with his old wound tearing in his back at every stride like a hook being pulled out of a fish, and he snatched a boathook off a swordless man as he passed him without breaking stride. It was a long ugly pole of ash, twelve feet of it, with an iron hook and spike at the end, a thing for fending ships off quays. It was not a lance. It would do.

"*Boy!*" he roared. "*Down!*"

Marcus turned. He saw. He did not go down; of course he did not. He set his feet and raised his sword, the fool, the damned brave fool, and the young rider's lance came down level at his chest.

Roderic got there first.

He did not try to stop the horse. No man on foot stops a horse. He went in from the side, low, inside the reach of the lance, and planted the butt of the boathook in the sand at his heel and brought the iron head up under the rider's arm as the horse went by; and the horse went on, and the rider did not. The hook took him under the armpit and the whole weight of the gallop went into the pole and up through Roderic's arms and into his shoulders and his back and the old wound, and the young Tharsian came out of the saddle backward like a cork out of a jar, and went over the horse's tail, and hit the black sand with a sound Roderic felt in his own teeth.

The other three riders swerved. Two went wide. One came on.

Roderic let go of the boathook and drew his sword and stood in front of the boy with it, panting, with the red nail going in his back and his vision gone white at the edges. The third rider came down on them with his lance aimed at Roderic now, and Roderic knew, quite calmly, that he was too slow; that he was fifty-two years old and had nine wounds and a back like a broken stair, and that he was going to die on a black beach in a fishing town in front of a pack of thieves, with no songs at all.

A shield came across in front of him.

It was Rufo's. The optio had come out of the end of his own line at a run, with the condemned shield of the Fourteenth on his arm, and he threw himself in front of the two of them and took the lance on the shield's face at full gallop. The shield split. The lance-head went through the boss and through the oak and stopped a finger's width short of Rufo's chest, and the optio was flung back into Roderic and the both of them went down in a heap on the sand, and the horse went over them, and a hoof came down so close to Roderic's head that he felt the sand it threw sting his cheek.

When he got up, the rider was down. Marcus had him. The boy had gone under the horse's belly as it passed and cut the girth, or the rider's leg, or both, and the man was on the sand with his foot caught in his own stirrup, being dragged, screaming, along the water's edge by his panicked horse.

The last two riders wheeled away along the beach the way they had come, and were gone round the end of the dunes.

"*Rufo!*" Marcus was on his knees by the optio. "Rufo!"

Decimus Rufo sat up. He looked at the lance-head sticking through the front of his shield, a finger from his heart. He looked at it for a long time. Then he looked at Roderic, and at the boy.

"Fourteenth," he said hoarsely, and tapped the faded red *XIV* on his split shield with one finger. "Condemned stores. Lucky I didn't get one of the good ones, General. It'd have gone straight through."

Marcus laughed. It came out of him in a bark, startled, like a dog surprised by its own voice, and then he was on his feet and turning to the line again.

Roderic stayed where he was a moment longer, on one knee on the black sand, getting his breath back. His back was screaming. His hands were shaking. He looked down the beach.

The first rider's horse was standing at the water's edge a dozen paces off, with its reins trailing in the surf and its saddle empty, blowing and trembling. A grey mare, Tharsian bred, deep in the chest and long in the leg, with a red cloth under her saddle and a brand on her quarter shaped like a closed hand.

*Tullus,* thought Roderic, out of nowhere. *Tullus the horse-thief. I told him if there were horses on Cyrene they'd be his to steal.*

Tullus was in the back of the witch's weather-book, in the boy's bad blotted hand.

Roderic got up. He walked down to the water, slowly, the way you walk to a frightened horse, talking to her in the low nonsense voice that he had used since he was six years old and that had never once failed him; and she put her ears back, and then forward, and let him take the reins.

---

There is a moment in a fight, if a man has fought enough of them, when he feels it turn. It is not a thing he sees. It is like the tide. Nothing changes and then everything has changed, and the water is going the other way.

Roderic felt it as he swung up into the Tharsian saddle. He felt it in his knees and in the mare's back under him and in the noise of the beach. The dead were down. The cohort's line was moving now, slowly, up the black sand toward the dunes, behind the boy and the split shields, and on the crest of the dunes the crossbowmen were not kneeling anymore. They were looking back over their shoulders toward the town.

The officer on the horse was shouting at them. The man in the long coat with the papers had gone.

Roderic looked at the line of the dunes. He looked at the crossbowmen. He counted them: nine, and an officer, and a gap between the end of their file and the end of the dunes that a horse could go through in three strides. He knew exactly what would happen if a horse went through it. He had known it since before the boy was born.

His back screamed at him. He ignored it, as he had ignored it on the Long Field.

He looked down at the boy, who was looking up at him from the sand with an expression Roderic had never seen on his face before, part alarm and part something that was very nearly a grin.

"Don't," said Marcus.

"Centurion," said Roderic of the Long Field, with great dignity, "they will write *songs*."

He put his heels to the grey mare and went up the beach.

He did not shout as he went. He meant to. He had always shouted, all his life, he had announced every charge from the first, *The Long Field! The Long Field and the king!*, so that the men behind him would know and the men before him would know and the people watching from the walls would know. But the breath was not in him. His back had it. So he went up the black sand and over the crest of the dune in silence, low on the mare's neck, with his sword out flat along her shoulder like a lance; and the crossbowmen heard him only when he was among them, and by then it was done.

He went through the end of their file like a stone through a window. He cut down one man. The mare broke another under her chest.

And the world went white.

It came down out of nowhere a spear's length in front of the mare's nose: a crack like the sky splitting, and a crossbowman who had been rising to aim at him simply lifted off his feet in a blaze of light and was flung down the back of the dune, smoking. The grey mare screamed and went straight up on her hind legs. Roderic felt himself going backward out of the saddle, with his sword in one hand and nothing in the other and his old wound tearing like wet canvas, and he got a fistful of mane at the last moment and hung there, and the mare came down, and he was still on her. Only just.

The silence of his charge broke. It broke in the one shout he gave all that day, and it was not *The Long Field*.

"*Sunstone rot your eyes, woman,*" he roared back down the beach, "*I was STANDING there!*"

The rest did not wait. They threw down their bows and ran, back over the dunes and down into the lanes of the town toward the fort on the headland, red cloaks flying, the officer on his horse galloping in front of them all; and Roderic drew rein on the crest of the dune with the grey mare blowing and dancing under him, and looked down into Cyrene.

Smoke. Steam. Black ribs of burned hulls. Doors standing open on empty houses. A dead dog in a lane. Up on the headland, the fort, and the bell still going.

Behind him, on the beach, the cohort was cheering. He could hear them. Two hundred thieves, cheering an old man on a stolen horse.

He did not turn round. His eyes were stinging. It was the sulphur, he told himself. It was the damned sulphur in this place.

When he did turn, at last, and looked down, the boy was not looking at him. Marcus was standing on the beach with the cohort around him, with his helmet off and his dark hair plastered to his skull with sweat, looking past Roderic, past the dunes, up at the fort on the headland. Not at Roderic at all.

*Not today, then,* Roderic thought, and was ashamed of the relief in it. *You'll ask me another day.*

---

**MARCUS**

The town was holding its breath.

He felt it the moment they came down off the dunes into the first lane. It was a narrow street of fishers' houses, low, stone-built, with roofs of turf and black slate, and every door in it was shut or broken. Steam came up out of the gutters. The whole island's drains ran warm, Nerva had said; in winter the children of Cyrene sat in the gutters to keep their feet from freezing. There were no children now. There was no one. There was a cart with one wheel off, and a scatter of herring dried to leather on a rack, and on every second door, at the height of a man's chest, a mark in white chalk.

He stopped at the first of them. Roderic, on the grey mare, stopped behind him.

It was a neat mark. Three short lines, and a long one, and under them a number, *2*. On the next door it was the same, with a *4*. On the one after, *1*.

"Tally," said Roderic.

"Of what?"

But he knew, as he said it, and the old man did not have to answer. Those were the doors where the dead had been. Somebody had gone down this lane with a stick of chalk, a clerk in a long coat, and counted, and written it down, and come back with the means to make them walk. *Two, in this one. Four, in that. One.*

He thought of the woman in the fishwife's apron in the oar-port. The boy of twelve. The shipwright with the adze. *Clock, clock.*

He did not let himself put a fist through the door. He walked on.

---

They found the boy in the net-lofts.

Atticus's slingers found him, rather, and nearly killed him. A shape moved in the dark of a loft door over the lane, and three slings came up, whirling, and the shape cried out, high and cracked, "*Don't! Don't, I'm from here!*" and came down the outside ladder so fast it nearly fell.

It was a boy of twelve or thirteen, barefoot, in a fisher's smock that had been white once and was now no color at all. He was thin as a gull's leg and filthy, with salt sores at the corners of his mouth and a gutting knife in his fist that he held out in front of him in both hands, point up, as though it were a holy sign and would ward off evil. His eyes went over the cohort. Over their condemned shields, the old red *XIV*. Over the old man on the Tharsian horse. They came to Marcus, and stopped there, and widened.

"You're them," the boy said. "From the sea. In the night." He swallowed. "We saw the lights. Under the water. My gran said it was the Deep come to take the town. She sat up all night with the lamp lit, saying the names."

"It wasn't the Deep," Marcus said. "It was a woman with a glass stick. What's your name?"

"Tam." The knife wavered. "Tam, son of Orso. Orso Netmender. Are you Aeridor?"

"Some of us."

"Did the king send you?"

Marcus thought about that.

"The king sent us," he said at last. "He didn't expect us to get here. Put the knife down, Tam. We're not here to hurt anyone in this town."

The boy did not put the knife down. He looked at Marcus a long moment with the hard old eyes that children get in a sacked town; and then he looked past him, up the hill, toward the waterfront and the round squat tower at the eastern end of it, with its red banner hanging limp and its bolt-thrower on the roof.

"Are you going to take the ports?"

"Yes."

"That one first?"

"That one first."

"You'll go at the front of it," Tam said. "Up the quay. They'll see you coming the whole length. There's ten of them in there, and the big bow." He shifted his feet. "My da used to mend nets for the customs men in that one. There's a way up the back. From the sea side. There's a stair cut in the rock under the tower for the boats to come in at, and an iron door at the top of it, and at low water you can walk out to the bottom of the stair on the weed-rocks without your feet getting wet. The Tharsians don't know about the water. They keep the door barred, but they don't watch it, because when they came the tide was in."

Marcus looked at him.

He heard, very faintly, behind his own voice in his head, another voice. Patient, amused, a decade older than his, in a muddy camp with the rain coming down. *A legion is two thousand men who all know something you don't, runner. Your job is to find out what. Ask them. Ask every man you meet. You'd be amazed what a cook knows.*

"Tam," he said. "What can you do that I can't?"

The boy blinked at him.

"I can find the weed-rocks in the dark," he said slowly. "And I know when the tide turns. And I can climb anything." He hesitated. "And I know which of them hit my gran."

"Is the tide out now?"

Tam looked at the sky. At the steam rising from the gutter. He sniffed, exactly as Nerva had sniffed over the lead.

"Going out," he said. "Low water in about a glass."

"Then show me," said Marcus.

---

They went down to the weed-rocks under the eastern tower with twenty men. Marcus would have taken fewer, but Rufo would not hear of it, and the optio stood on that the way he stood behind his split shield, immovably, until Marcus gave way. Twenty, then: Rufo and a dozen of his shields, and Atticus with four slingers, and Spurius Lento, called Fingers, with his roll of picks and wires. And Tam in front, barefoot, going over the black and green slime of the weed-rocks as lightly as a cat goes along a wall.

The rest of the cohort he sent to the quay, under Roderic, with orders to go slowly and loudly and stay out of bolt-range, and make the tower look at them. Roderic had grinned at that. *Be a nuisance in plain sight. I've been doing that since before your father was crowned.*

The rocks were slick as soap. The weed on them was warm, which was strange, and stank of the spring, and popped under his boots. Twice he went down to one knee. Once a cohort man went in up to his waist in a pool between the rocks, and came up spitting and swearing in a whisper, and Rufo hit him on the back of the head, not hard. Above them the tower rose up out of the rock like a stump out of a hillside, old grey stone streaked white with gulls, and on its roof Marcus could hear the Tharsians talking. He could not hear the words. Their speech was close kin to the isles' own, but rounder, with the ends of the words swallowed; it came down to him over the sound of the sea like the talk of men in a next room.

They were laughing at something. One of them was laughing at something on the quay.

*Good,* he thought. *Roderic's being a nuisance.*

The stair was where the boy had said it would be. A flight of steps cut into the living rock, slimed and treacherous and hollowed in the middle by two hundred years of customs men's boots, climbing up out of the water to a small iron door in the tower's foot. Fingers went up it first, on his toes, with Tam behind him. Marcus went third.

The door was barred inside. Fingers did not try the lock. He looked at the hinges instead, and grunted softly, and took a short iron bar and a little pot of grease out of his roll, and began to work at the pins.

"Eleven years in the harbor prison," he whispered over his shoulder. "And not one of 'em ever thought to ask me what I was in for."

The upper pin came out with a small wet squeak. The lower pin came out. Fingers set his shoulder to the hinge side of the door and leaned, and the whole door came away from the frame on that side, still barred, still locked, hanging off its bar like a broken wing, and there was a dark stair behind it, going up, and the smell of men and cold food and lamp-smoke.

Marcus went through first. Rufo tried to stop him. Marcus did not let him.

---

He would think about that afterward. He would think about it on the roof, with the banner in the boy's hands, and he would know that Rufo had been right, and that a commander who goes first through every door is only a man looking for a quicker stair. But at the time it did not feel like a choice. It felt like the stair at the bottom of the world, with the dark at the top and his men behind him, and he had been carried off that stair once already.

It was a narrow stair, winding up the inside of the tower wall, too narrow for two men abreast, and halfway up it there was a Tharsian coming down.

He was not a big man. He was older than Marcus had expected, with grey in his short beard, and he had a bowl of something in his hands, porridge or gruel, steaming. He was coming down to the storeroom, perhaps. Or to the privy. He came round the turn of the stair with the bowl held carefully in front of him and his eyes on it, so as not to spill, and looked up, and saw Marcus a step below him with a drawn sword.

They looked at each other.

It was the first living man Marcus had faced in three years. He had a moment, no more, to see everything about him: the grey in his beard, and the burst veins in his nose, and the scar through one eyebrow, and the iron ring on his thumb that archers wear, and the bowl. Something dried on the man's sleeve that might have been gruel or might have been something else. A face of fifty years, tired and heavy and surprised, a face like a hundred faces in any legion's mess-tent. And Marcus thought, very clearly, *Did you hit Tam's gran?* And then, *It doesn't matter. It doesn't matter what you did.*

The man opened his mouth to shout.

Marcus put the sword into him under the breastbone, up, the way Tiberius had taught them on the training shore with straw men and then not with straw men. The bowl went down the stair, ringing. The man fell against him, heavy, and his breath came out over Marcus's face, and it smelled of gruel and of onions, and then he did not breathe again.

His hands were steady. Marcus noticed it as he eased the dead man down onto the steps. They were as steady as they had been in the oar-port. Steady as a carpenter's.

*So that's what it takes,* he thought. *Not wine. This.*

He did not like the thought. He set it aside for later, with Alana's sickness and Iason's grief and the ninety-one names, and went on up the stair.

---

It did not take long after that. It never did, when the door was open.

There were nine more of them in the tower. Three in the guardroom on the first floor, at a table, with dice; they had heard the bowl on the stair and were half out of their chairs when Rufo came through the door behind his split shield like a bull through a gate. Two on the second floor, asleep. Four on the roof, at the bolt-thrower, laughing at Roderic, who was riding up and down the quay just out of bowshot on his grey mare, waving his sword at them and shouting something about their mothers.

The four on the roof turned round when the trapdoor came up. They did not have time to do much else. Atticus's slingers came up through the trap behind Marcus, and the slings went round in that close space with a sound like angry bees, and the stones did not miss at six paces.

One of the four was still alive at the end, on his knees by the bolt-thrower with his hands up and blood running out of his hair. He was hardly older than Tam. He was saying something over and over in his round Tharsian speech, and it took Marcus a moment to hear the words under the accent. *I yield. I yield. I yield.*

"Bind him," Marcus said. "Put him with the sleepers."

Rufo looked at him.

"Bind him," said Marcus again.

Rufo bound him.

Marcus walked to the parapet. The whole of Cyrene lay below him: the town, the bay, the burned slipways steaming, the long arms of the moles with their two squat towers, the far tower at the western end of the waterfront. And above it all, on the black headland, the fort, with its one square tower. The bell was still ringing up there. He could see it now, a dark shape swinging in the open arch at the top of the tower, small as a thimble at this distance.

On the quay, the cohort had stopped. They were looking up at him. Roderic, on the grey mare, had stopped riding up and down and was looking up at him too.

Above Marcus's head, the red banner of Tharsis hung limp from its pole. Cloth of good dark red, heavy, wet with the fog, with a white closed hand stitched at its center. The same hand that was branded on the grey mare's quarter. He reached for the halyard.

"Can I?"

Tam was beside him. He had come up through the trapdoor last, after the fighting, and he stood at the foot of the pole with his gutting knife in his belt now and his thin chest going in and out. He was not looking at Marcus. He was looking up at the banner.

"Please," the boy said. "Highness. Can I?"

Marcus let go of the halyard.

Tam did not use the halyard. He went up the pole hand over hand, the way he went up the net-loft ladders, the way he had said he could climb anything; and at the top he took out the gutting knife and sawed through the cords that held the banner, one, two, three, and the red cloth slid off the pole and came down in a long slow heavy fall, turning over once in the air, and landed across the bolt-thrower like a cloak thrown over a chair.

On the quay below, the cohort began to cheer. Marcus heard Roderic's great voice in the middle of it. And then he heard another sound, under the cheering, from the town. He could not tell at first what it was.

Tam slid down the pole. He stood at the foot of it, shaking now, white, with the knife in his hand.

"I didn't ask," he said. "I'm sorry. I should have asked you first. Before. About the knife. And going up."

"You did ask," said Marcus. "You asked me on the weed-rocks. You asked me in the lane." He bent and picked up the banner. It was heavy and wet and smelled of mildew. He put it into the boy's arms. "Listen to me, Tam son of Orso. Look down there."

The boy looked down. Marcus looked with him.

All along the waterfront, in the lanes of the town under the tower, the doors were opening. Not all of them. A few. A door, and then another door, and faces in the doors, gaunt and grey and frightened, men and women and children, looking up at the round tower at the end of the quay with the red banner gone from its pole and a barefoot boy of Cyrene standing on its roof with the cloth in his arms.

"They'll tell this," Marcus said. "Tonight, and for the rest of their lives. And when they do, they'll say Aeridor came back. They'll say a prince came out of the sea with an army, and they'll get the army wrong, and they'll get me wrong. Let them. But you remember, Tam. The weed-rocks were yours. The stair was yours. And the rope was your idea." He put a hand on the boy's thin shoulder. "That's how this works, from now on. It's yours. You only let us help."

Tam looked at him. Then down at the town. His mouth worked, and nothing came out of it, and he clutched the wet red banner to his chest like a child holding a cat.

And up on the headland, all at once, the bell stopped ringing.

Marcus had heard bells in his head for three days and nights. He had heard the bells of the Old Hill on the *Clemency*'s deck, a hundred miles from any bell. He had heard this one since dawn, and had stopped hearing it, the way a man stops hearing the sea. He heard it now, by its absence. The silence came down over the bay like a dropped cloak, and in the silence he could hear the surf, and the gulls, and the steam hissing in the gutters of the town, and somebody down in the lanes, a woman, weeping.

He looked up at the fort. There was no one in the arch of the bell-tower now. The bell hung still.

*They've stopped calling for help,* he thought. *Because they've understood that none is coming.*

Then, after a long moment, he thought: *Neither is ours.*

Below him, along the waterfront of Cyrene, one after another, the doors went on opening.



---

# Chapter Seven: The Heart-Stone

**ALANA**

By noon Cyrene had given back three of its four ports, and she had stopped counting anything but the casks.

She heard the rest of it in pieces, the way news comes to a hospital. The two towers on the moles had been cut off at the ends of their long stone arms the moment the eastern tower fell, and their crews had not waited to be killed. They had gone down their sea-stairs into their boats and rowed for the headland under the slingers' stones, and climbed into the fort by its own water-gate, and left their bolt-throwers to the gulls. The western tower had held out an hour longer. Then Roderic had ridden up to its door on his stolen grey, alone, with his helmet off and his sword sheathed, and told the men inside in a voice they must have heard in the fort that the witch who had burned the Kettle Bank was standing behind him with a glass stick, and she was *very* tired, and she did not like to be kept waiting.

She had not been standing behind him. She had been on the beach, on her knees in the wreck of the *Clemency*'s stern cabin, counting oil. But the Tharsians had come out with their hands up, nine of them, and the General had come back down the waterfront looking so pleased with himself that she had not had the heart to scold him for the lie.

*Twenty-three casks.* Thirty-one bought on credit in the king's name, and eight poured over the Kettle Bank, and twenty-three left, and two of those cracked in the beaching and leaking slowly into the bilge so that the whole stern of the old ship smelled like a chandler's. Twenty-one, then. She had written it in the weather-book under the drowned of Cyrene, and stared at it, and done the sum again.

Her right hand hurt. The rag on it had stuck to the burn in the night, and she had not taken it off, because she knew what she would find under it and there was nothing she could do about it on a beached prison hulk. It throbbed in time with her heart. She had stopped noticing the nosebleeds.

The fort was the fourth port, and the last, and it was not going to fall to an old man's bluff.

---

They found the healer in the steam-box house.

Alana had gone up from the beach with the cohort's wounded, because the barber Hostus had asked for her, and because she wanted, for an hour, to be useful in some way that was not fire. The cohort had taken over the slipways as the only open ground in the town that could be held. The wounded lay on the warm stone of the second slip in the lee of a half-built hull, a dozen of them, with bolts in their feet and their shoulders and one in his belly who would not live till dark; and Hostus went among them with his curved needle and his disapproving face, and Alana went behind him with a pot of hot water and a pair of hands, and neither of them said much.

Then the door of the steam-box house opened, at the head of the slip, and a woman came out of the steam.

Alana would try, afterward, to put it down in the weather-book as it had happened, plainly, as one writes up an experiment. She never managed it. Whatever she wrote came out sounding like the lives of the saints.

The long shed had vents along its roof-ridge to let the steam out, and the noon light came down through them in slanting bars, and the steam rolled out of the open door into the light and lit up gold; and the woman walked out of the middle of it. For one heartbeat the light was behind her, and the steam around her, and Alana, who did not believe in anything she could not weigh, saw a figure out of the painted windows of the chapel of the Sun at Corvus: a woman standing in a haze of gold with light coming off her edges.

Then she stepped out of the light, and she was only a woman, and that was not much better.

She was beautiful. There was no use pretending otherwise, and Alana, who prided herself on calling a thing by its right name, did not try. Her hair was the color of the Heart-Stones in the old drawings, honey and gold, and it fell loose and heavy to her waist in long waves, unbound, as no respectable woman of the isles wore it outside her own bedchamber. Her skin was very fair. Her face was the kind the court painters of the capital spent their lives attempting and getting wrong: wide-browed, fine-boned, calm, with a mouth made for gentleness and eyes of a clear grey-green, like shallow water over pale sand. At the throat of her robe there was a high stiff collar worked in gold thread, the Vigil's sun-thread, and in the hollow of it a single small stone of pale blue glass.

The robe had been white silk. Fine stuff, the stuff of a great house. It hung on her now in pieces. One sleeve had been torn away at the shoulder, so that her arm and shoulder were bare to the cold air. The bodice was ripped across below the collar, and the halves of it held together only by the gold-threaded girdle at her waist and a shipwright's nail pushed through both edges like a brooch. The skirt was split up one side almost to the hip, so that when she walked her leg showed through the rent, long and pale and smeared to the knee with somebody else's dried blood. What sleeve remained was rolled to the elbow, and her forearms were stained to the wrist with old blood, brown, the stain that does not come out.

A lady of the capital would have died of shame to be seen so. Alana had met a great many of them, and she was quite sure of it.

The woman did not seem to know it was happening. She did not gather the silk together. She did not fold her arms across herself, or turn her shoulder, or so much as glance down. She came out of the steam as though she wore a high priestess's cope, and her eyes went straight past Alana to the wounded lying on the slip, and moved along them, one to the next, and Alana saw that she was counting them, and sorting them, and had finished both before she spoke.

Hostus, who had seen a hundred drunks naked in Tanner's Lane and never turned a hair, went red to the ears and found something urgent to look at on the stones.

"Master barber," the woman said. Her voice was low and clear, with the long careful vowels of the old houses of the capital, which Alana had last heard across the high table of the Lesser Hall. It was a gentle voice. It did not ask permission. "It's only cloth. Look at the boy behind me instead. His hand was crushed under a beam at dawn, and he is twelve, and he has been very brave about it for a long time."

Hostus looked up. Behind her, in the doorway, a boy sat on an upturned bucket holding a wrapped and swollen hand in his lap, white-lipped.

"You'll be the surgeon," the woman said.

"Barber," said Hostus.

"Good. Barbers wash their hands." She smiled at him, a small warm smile that did something to the barber's long disapproving face that Alana had not thought possible. "Come with me, both of you. I have forty-one people in that shed and six who need cutting, and until this morning I had a shipwright's knife and a pot of spring water and whatever hours the garrison did not want. I am very glad to see you." As Hostus went past her through the door she put out her hand and touched his arm, lightly, at the elbow. "Thank you. Truly."

Alana did not go in at once. She stood in the doorway with the hot steam on her face and looked at the woman, because she could not, for the moment, do anything else.

And so she saw the bruises.

Close to, in the plain light, they were not hard to see. A bruise along the left cheekbone, high, under the eye, gone from black to the yellow-green of an old one. A split at the corner of the mouth, half healed. And on both wrists, below the rolled silk, half hidden under the brown of the old blood, a ring of marks that were not the stain: dark, close-set, oval, each the size of a fingertip. The prints of hands. Somebody had held her by the wrists, and held hard, and more than once.

The beauty made them worse. That was the thing Alana could not put down afterward. On a plainer face they would have been bruises. On this one they were a crime written across the page of a holy book, by a hand that had wanted it to be read.

There was another thought under that one, small and unworthy and quick as a cutpurse. *Marcus will see her.* Alana caught it and looked at it and was so ashamed of it that she felt her face burn in the steam. She set it aside, with everything else, for later.

"You're Vigil," she said. "The collar. That's the sun-thread."

"I trained with them. I took no vows." The woman looked at Alana in turn: at the charred Corvus silk, and the oil in its hem, and the burned hand in its filthy rag. Something moved in the grey-green eyes. "And you are the one who lit the sea."

"Yes."

"We saw it from here, through the roof-vents. All night." She was quiet a moment. "I knew a great many of the people who were standing on that bank. I washed some of them, before. The children, mostly." Her voice did not shake. "I'm glad it was fire. It is the only rite there is for that, and they had waited long enough for one."

"I didn't do it as a rite."

"No," said the woman. "But it was one, all the same. The Sun doesn't ask what we meant by it." She held out her bloody hand, palm up, in the old manner of the southern isles, the way one greets an equal. "Liora."

Alana took it with her left.

"Alana. Of Corvus." And, because it seemed important, though she could not have said why: "There isn't any Corvus anymore."

"There isn't much Cyrene." Liora did not let go of her hand at once. Her fingers were warm and very steady. "We came south after Hollowmere, on the Vigil's hospital ship, with eighty of the worst of Prince Titus's wounded. The springs here are good for burns, and for wounds that won't close. That is why we came. The fort fell nine days later." She said the next part in exactly the same voice, gentle and level, as she might have told a family that the fever had turned. "They killed the eighty in their beds at the Baths, and raised them, and walked them into the sea. They kept me. A healer is useful. I mended their men in the fort, day and night, every cut and fever and crushed hand, until I could not stand, and when I could not stand they let me kneel. At the end of each day, if I had been obedient, they brought me down here and locked me in with the town's hurt, and I mended those with what was left. I was not always obedient. They brought me down anyway. Even Tharsians are not such fools as to waste a healer."

Alana looked at the bruise on her cheek. She could not help it. She saw Liora see her look.

"Did they hurt you?" Alana said, very low.

Liora did not look away. That was the thing Alana would remember: the grey-green eyes holding hers, clear and level and entirely unafraid, as though it were Alana who might need to be steadied, and Liora who would do the steadying.

"They kept me useful," she said. "In the day, at the healing. And some of them came down to the steam-box house at night, after. Not for healing." She let that stand for the space of a breath, no longer. "I won't speak of it, Alana. Not to you, and not to your prince, and not to anyone. Not because I can't. Because it is mine, and they have had enough of what is mine." Her voice was still gentle. It did not bend at all. "Please don't ask me again."

"I won't," said Alana.

"Thank you." And then, as if nothing at all had been said, she turned Alana's right hand over in her own, and looked at the rag on it, and at the brown edge of the burn creeping out from under the rag, and her face changed. Not much. The look of a master mason who has found a crack in a wall he was told was sound. "Now. Come inside. I want to see that."

"It's nothing."

"It is going bad," said Liora, "and you know it is, because you are a scholar and you have not looked at it for a day, which is what scholars do with a thing they already understand." She tightened her grip, very slightly. It was not a request. "Come inside. Five minutes. Then you can go back to your prince and burn whatever else he needs burned."

Alana went inside.

---

It was in the steam-box house, with her hand in a bowl of hot salt water and the healer cutting the dead skin away with a shipwright's knife, that she met the engineer.

He came in at the far end of the shed through a low iron door in the wall that she had taken for a furnace door, crawling, backward, on his hands and knees, and stood up with soot on his face and his shirt black with wet to the shoulders and a wax tablet clutched against his chest as another man might hold a wounded child. He was perhaps thirty, perhaps a little more, and he looked fifty. Gaunt to the bone, with a jaw that needed a razor and eyes red-rimmed and sunk in their sockets like a man who had not slept in a week, and a pair of round glass lenses in leather frames pushed up on his forehead and fogged white with steam. His hands were the worst of him. Big hands, made for work, and the work had eaten them: the palms raw and weeping, two nails gone black, the knuckles split and scabbed and split again. There was an iron ring round his left ankle with a link of broken chain still hanging from it. When he stood still he swayed, very slightly, the way a mast sways at anchor.

He looked down the length of the shed, at the pallets and the children and the barber, and at Alana with her hand in the bowl.

"You're the army," he said.

"Part of it."

"Good. Where's the rest? I need to talk to whoever's going to try to take that fort, before he does something stupid and expensive." He wiped his hands on his wet shirt, which only made them blacker, and came down the shed toward her. "Gaius. I'm an engineer. I was building a gantry for the third slip when the dead came over the hill, and the garrison has kept me alive for fifteen days because nobody else on this island understands their own steam-boxes. They wanted their ships finished. Twenty hours in the day on the slips, with a quaestor's man behind me and a rod, and four hours in a cell." He said it the way he might have given the weight of a beam. "The captain let me keep my books. My tables, my load-sums, two volumes of Anaxis on arches. He said an engineer without his tables was a waste of bread. It's the only kindness anyone in a red cloak has done me, and I hated him for it, because it meant I could work faster." He looked down at his ruined hands as if they were tools someone had lent him and he had misused. "So I worked slower, in ways they couldn't see. Every hull I touched has a fault in it only I know about." He set the wax tablet down on the edge of Liora's table beside the bowl. "That's the fort."

Alana looked.

It was a plan. Cut into the wax with a stylus, small and exact and beautiful, the work of a hand that did not waste a line: the walls, the gate, the one square tower, the well, the stair down to the water-gate on the seaward side. And under the fort, drawn in a different, heavier line, a channel. It began somewhere under the tower, and came down out of the headland beneath the town, and ran under the slipways, and ended—she looked up at the low iron door he had crawled out of.

"Here," said Gaius. "The fort's built over the spring-head. That's why there's a fort there at all. The spring comes up in the rock under the tower and they used to send the water down through this culvert to the yards for the steam-boxes and the town drains. You can't heat a plank-box with a fire hot enough to do any good without burning the oak, but the island does it for free, all day and all night, if you pipe it." He tapped the line in the wax. "It's a stone drain about as high as a man's chest. It runs from the back of that door to a grate in the floor of the fort's undercroft. I've been in it as far as the grate. Four times. At night." He looked at her steadily. "The grate's old iron, set in lead. The garrison doesn't know it's there. Nobody's been down that drain in forty years but me and the rats."

"Why haven't you gone through it?" Alana said.

"Because I'm an engineer, not a regiment. What would I do on the other side, measure them?" He took the tablet back. "And because the water. It runs a hand deep, all the time, and at the upper end it's hot enough to scald a pig. They lock me at night in the old sluice-house under the yards, because it's the only stone room down there with one door. They never counted the sluice as a door. I've been closing it from inside at night to drop the water enough to crawl, and opening it again before dawn so the steam-boxes don't go cold and the garrison doesn't come down to see why." He paused. "And because I didn't know what was on the other side of the grate. I do now. I heard it, the last time. Someone's working down there. Under the tower. There's a chisel going, all day. Tink. Tink. Tink. Like a man cutting stone."

Alana took her hand out of the bowl.

Liora, beside her, said, "I haven't finished."

"You have," said Alana. "I'm sorry. Bind it, please. Quickly." She looked at the engineer. "The spring comes up under the tower."

"Yes."

"Then that's where the stone is."

He looked at her. He had grey eyes, she noticed, very pale, the color of the Grey itself, and they did not move off her face as she spoke; and she saw the moment he understood, because the eyes went still.

"The Heart-Stone," he said. "The island's Heart-Stone. They're cutting it."

---

Marcus was at the head of the hill, below the fort's gate, in the shelter of a ruined chapel of the Sun, with Rufo and Roderic and a dozen of the cohort crouched behind the broken wall. The fort was two bowshots above them up a steep and naked slope of black rock and dead heather. Its gate was shut. It was a good gate, oak and iron, between two squat towers, and on the wall over it Alana could see red cloaks moving, and the long shapes of crossbows, and the round iron heads of men looking down.

Two of the cohort lay on the open slope halfway up to it, not moving, where they had tried to rush it with a ladder from one of the net-lofts. The ladder lay beside them. It had a crossbow bolt through one of its rungs.

"Bar on the inside, and iron straps," Rufo was saying as she came up. "We could go at it with a ram all afternoon under that wall and they'd shoot us down like crows off a fence."

Marcus looked round at her. He did not say anything. He did not have to. She saw his eyes go to the glass rod in her belt, and then to her bandaged hand, and then away, and she understood that he would not ask, because of the hand.

"Stand clear of the chapel door," Alana said.

She went out from behind the broken wall onto the open slope. She heard Marcus say her name, sharp, and Roderic swear, and she did not stop. Twenty paces. Thirty. A bolt went past her head with a sound like a hornet and struck the rock behind her. She stopped where she could see the whole face of the gate, the black oak and the iron straps and the squat towers either side, and the red cloaks on the wall above, and drew the rod.

Fire would not do it. Fire would only blacken the oak. Lightning would run down the iron and into the ground and do nothing at all.

The *runeblast* was not a thing the masters of Corvus taught. It was a thing they had argued about, for forty years, in the third chair's seminar, and forbidden, and written down very carefully so that no one would ever do it by accident. A rune that did not make fire or light or cold, but only *force*, folded in on itself and folded again and then let go all at once, the way a man lets go of a drawn bow. She had done it twice in her life. Once in the academy's cellars at sixteen, to see if she could, and it had cost her a wall and a term's suspension. Once at Hollowmere, on a cart full of the dead, and she had not been able to hear properly for three days.

She drew it on the air in front of the gate. It was not one stroke but nine, each inside the last, and they hung in the air after the rod had passed, glowing faintly, like the afterimage of a lamp. Her hand screamed. Her nose began to bleed again, warm, over her lip. A bolt struck the rock at her feet and threw stone chips into her shins and she did not move.

She spoke the last stroke's name.

The air in front of the gate *bent*. She saw it bend, like the air over a forge; and then the whole of it went out from her in a single silent shove, and struck.

The sound came after. A great flat boom that she felt in her ribs and her teeth and the soles of her feet, and the gate of the fort of Cyrene jumped in its frame like a door kicked by a giant. Splinters the length of a man's arm flew out of it. One of its iron straps snapped and whipped away across the slope singing. And on the wall above, the force came up the face of the stone and over the parapet like a wave over a harbor wall, and took the men standing there; and Alana watched, quite calmly, seven or eight red cloaks go up and backward off the wall-walk into the courtyard behind, with their crossbows wheeling over their heads, as a gust takes washing off a line.

When the dust cleared, the gate was still standing.

It was split from top to bottom down its left leaf, and sagging on its hinges, and the bar behind it must have been bent like a bow; but it was standing. And the two squat towers either side of it stood exactly as they had stood for two hundred years, without a mark on them, and out of the slits in them, after a moment, the bolts began to come again.

Alana sat down on the black rock, quite suddenly, as she had sat down on the *Clemency*'s deck.

Behind her she heard Gaius, in a voice of deep professional interest, say: "Granite. Dressed, and set in lime. You'll not shift that with anything short of the island turning over, lady. Do that once more and you'll bring the tower down on the gate and none of us will ever get in."

"I know," said Alana, to the slope. "I know. I was testing a hypothesis."

And from the chapel, as two of the cohort came out under their shields to drag her back behind the wall by her arms, she heard the prince say to the General, very dryly, not quite low enough:

"Remind me, Roderic. Remind me every morning. The lady hates me."

"Not a lady," said Alana, being dragged, without opening her eyes.

---


Afterward, when she could sit up, she told him, and Gaius told him, and the engineer laid his wax tablet on a fallen stone of the chapel's altar and showed him the drain.

He listened without interrupting. That was new. She remembered him across the table of the Lesser Hall, eight years ago, talking over everyone. He crouched by the altar stone with his helmet off and his dark hair flattened with sweat and his hands, she noticed, quite steady, and he listened to the engineer the way he had listened to the fisher-boy, and then he asked the question.

"How many can go up it?"

"Four," said Gaius. "Five at the most. It's a chest-high drain with a hand of near-scalding water in the bottom. Any more and the last ones will be breathing the first ones' steam."

"And the grate?"

"Old iron in lead. A man with the right tools and no conscience could have it out in half a glass."

"Fingers," said Marcus, and Spurius Lento, crouched behind the wall with his roll of picks under his arm, looked up with an expression of deep foreboding. "And the engineer, because he knows the way. And—"

"Me," said Alana.

He looked at her. She saw him want to say no. She saw it go through his face, quickly, the way a cloud goes over a field. Then she saw him put it away.

"Yes," he said. "You. If there's a chisel going under that tower, there's a man with it who knows how to make the dead walk. And you're the only one of us who knows what he knows." He looked at the culvert line on the wax. "And me."

"*No,*" said Roderic and Rufo together.

Marcus turned his head and looked at them, one and then the other. Something passed between the three of them that Alana did not understand. She saw the General's face, under its beard, go grey and fixed, the face of a man waiting for a blow.

"The gate's the gate, Centurion," said Rufo, very quietly. "They'll open it from inside or they won't. Either way, two hundred men are going to have to stand under that wall and take bolts while they wait for it. And they'll stand if they can see you. They won't if they can't." He did not look away. "You know it. You said it yourself on the deck. You stood on a box and told us we belonged to ourselves. Well, we do. And we'd like you where we can see you, please, sir, when the bolts come."

Marcus did not move for a long moment.

"The stair," he said at last. Alana did not know what he meant. She saw Roderic flinch. "The last time I wasn't where my men could see me, they were on a stair." He let out a long breath. "All right. All right, optio. The gate."

Then he looked at Alana, and she saw that he was afraid, and that he was not going to say so.

"Iason," he said, without turning his head. "You know the yards."

"I know the drain," said Iason. He was crouched at the end of the wall, with his sword across his knees, and he had not spoken since the Kettle Bank. "I used to swim in the outfall. Every apprentice did. Master Philon would beat us for it." His voice did not change. "I'll go."

"Four, then. Alana, the engineer, Fingers, Iason." Marcus stood up. "When the gate opens, we'll come. Until the gate opens, we'll be under the wall, making noise." He looked at Alana again. "Don't wait for us. Whatever's down there. Don't wait."

"I wasn't going to," said Alana.

---

The drain was worse than the engineer had said.

It was a tunnel of fitted stone, square, very old, not quite high enough to crawl in on hands and knees without scraping your spine along the roof, and the water ran down the floor of it a hand deep and so hot that within a dozen yards Alana could not feel her knees through her soaked robes, only a dull red heat, like kneeling on a stove. The air was worse than the water. It was the spring's own breath, sulphur and iron and steam, so thick in the dark that the little shuttered lantern Gaius carried in front made a ball of white fog and showed nothing.

They went up it in the dark, on their knees, in the steam, for what might have been half a glass and might have been an hour. Gaius in front with the lantern. Then Fingers, swearing under his breath, in a whisper, continuously and without repeating himself, which Alana found she was grateful for. Then Alana, with her bound hand held up out of the water against her chest and the glass rod thrust through her belt and two small stoppered jars of oil on cords around her neck, bumping against her ribs. Then Iason, saying nothing at all.

Gaius had closed the sluice at the bottom before they went in. The water dropped, but only to half a hand, and it grew hotter as they climbed. Somewhere, in the black ahead, the island was breathing up out of the rock.

And somewhere, very faintly, through the stone, through the water, as Gaius had said, a sound. *Tink. Tink. Tink.*

Then the engineer stopped, and turned his head, and put the lantern's shutter down to a slit.

There was a grate above them. Alana could see it, a grid of black iron bars against a dim and flickering gold light, a hand's breadth over Gaius's head where the drain's roof opened upward into a shaft. Through the bars came warm air and the smell of hot lamp-oil, and the chisel, loud now, close. *Tink.* And a voice. A man's voice, speaking quietly, steadily, in the round Tharsian accent. Not to anyone. To himself, the way a clerk talks under his breath while he adds a column.

"…seventeen. Eighteen. Mind the fault, there, you'll split it. Nineteen…"

Fingers went up under the grate and looked at it for a long time with his face almost touching the iron. Then he opened his roll of picks in the hot dark, by feel, and took out a small sharp curved tool like a dentist's hook, and began, very gently, to dig at the lead.

---

It took him less than half a glass. It felt like a night.

When the last bar came loose he held it, and lowered it into the drain, and looked down at Alana. His face was running with sweat. He nodded once.

She went up first. She had not discussed it with anyone. She simply went, past Gaius, with her good hand on the rim of the shaft and her knee on the engineer's shoulder, up through the hole where the grate had been, into the gold light.

The undercroft of the fort of Cyrene was a cave.

Men had squared its walls and set pillars in it, long ago, and laid a floor of flagstones, but it was a cave in the black rock of the headland all the same, low and round and long, lit by a dozen lamps set in niches, and so warm that the air was a soup. In the middle of it, at the far end, the floor fell away into a pool. The pool was steaming. Its water was clear as glass and blue at the bottom, a blue so deep it was almost black, and out of the middle of it, out of the living rock at the pool's heart, rose the Heart-Stone of Cyrene.

Alana had read of Heart-Stones all her life. She had seen the drawings in the Corvus library, before the fire, and the shard on its chain that the master pilots of the academy kept for their voyages. She had never seen one.

It was the size of a hayrick. A great slanting tooth of crystal standing up out of the pool, amber and gold, faceted like a gem and then not like a gem at all, broken and grown and broken again over ages into a thousand planes and points and shelves; and inside it, deep inside, there was light. Not a reflection of the lamps. Its own. A slow, warm, pulsing light, the color of honey held up to the sun, that came and went, came and went, like the breathing of something asleep. The water of the spring welled up around its foot and steamed off its flanks. The whole cave was gold with it.

And there was scaffolding against its side.

Rough planks lashed to poles. A ladder. Chisel scars all down the near face of the crystal, white and raw, where pieces had been cut away. On the flagstones at the edge of the pool, a row of small wooden crates, open, packed with straw, and in the straw, in every crate, wrapped each in a twist of linen, shards. Dozens of them. Each the length of a finger or a hand. Each glowing faintly through the linen like a coal through ash.

On the scaffold, a man was cutting another one.

He had his back to her. He was a narrow, stooping man in a long dark coat with the sleeves turned back, standing on the planks with a mallet in one hand and a fine chisel in the other, setting the chisel's edge with great care into a fault in the crystal; and on a stool beside the scaffold's foot there was a ledger, open, and a pen in a pot of ink, and a cup of wine.

"Twenty," he said, to himself. *Tink.*

Behind Alana, Gaius came up out of the drain, and then Fingers, and then Iason. She heard the engineer draw his breath.

The man on the scaffold heard it too. He turned round.

---

He was not old. That was her first surprise. Thirty-five, perhaps. A scholar's face, thin and pale and intelligent and tired, with ink on the side of his finger and a pair of reading lenses on a cord round his neck. The face of a dozen men she had sat beside at the long tables in the reading room at Corvus. He looked at the four of them coming up out of the floor of his cave, wet and steaming and filthy, and his first expression was not fear. It was annoyance. The look of a man interrupted in the middle of a line.

Then his eyes came to her robes, and he went quite still.

"Corvus silk," he said. "That's the last bolt. They said there was a girl who'd got out with it." His voice was soft and pleasant, a lecturer's voice, the accent rounding the words. "I wondered if it would be you, out on the bank. The fire. I said so to Captain Merula. I said, *that's not oil alone. Somebody's written on it.*" He set the mallet and the chisel down carefully on the plank. "Sabellius. Quaestor of the Fourth Survey. I have the honor of the inventory of this island."

"You're cutting the stone," Alana said.

"I'm *counting* it." He seemed, very faintly, offended. "Forty shards, at the King's order. Thirty-nine for the King's pilots and one for the Survey. When it's done there'll be no ship in Aeridor that can find Cyrene in the Grey without Tharsis's leave, and every ship of Tharsis's will find it in her sleep. That is all a stone is, you know. A claim. Somebody's name on the map." He came down the ladder, slowly, and stood at its foot by the stool with the ledger. "You are a long way from your library, little chair."

The name went into her like a needle.

"Don't call me that."

"It's what *she* calls you." He smiled. "In her letters. She writes to the Survey quite often. She takes an interest in our work. She asked to be told if you were ever found." He laid one hand, lightly, on the open ledger. "Shall I tell her you were?"

Alana raised the rod.

And the quaestor of the Fourth Survey said a word, one word, softly, and the dead in the dark at the back of the cave stood up.

---

She had not seen them. She would never forgive herself that. She had come up out of the floor and seen the Heart-Stone, and the crates, and the man, and she had not looked into the shadows behind the pillars at the back of the cave where the lamps did not reach; and they had been lying there, in a row, on the warm flagstones, a dozen of them, like men asleep in a barracks. And now they stood up. Not slowly, as the dead on the beach had come. All at once, at the word, like soldiers at a trumpet.

They were soldiers. They wore the scale and the blue cloaks of Aeridor, torn and black with old blood, and the crest of Cyrene's garrison on their shields, the old leaping dolphin: the men who had held this fort for the king, fifteen days ago, until it fell. The quaestor had kept them here in the warm, by the spring, to keep them fresh. *Stock,* thought Alana, insanely. *He's kept them as stock.*

"Walk," said the quaestor, without raising his voice. "Hold. Kill what lives."

They came out from behind the pillars.

Alana drew the fire rune.

Her burned hand screamed as the glass went hot in it and she held on. The hook, the cross-stroke, the closing turn; the air folded, the fire went out across the cave in a fist and struck the first two of the dead and they went up, burning, and fell. She drew it again. Her hand was a blaze of pain to the elbow. Again. *Four. Five.* The cave filled with smoke and the stink of it, and the dead came on through the smoke in their blue cloaks with their swords up, and the light of the burning ones ran gold and red over the Heart-Stone's thousand faces so that the whole cave seemed to be on fire.

Beside her Fingers had his long knife out and was backing, backing, his face white. Gaius had snatched up the quaestor's mallet from the plank and was standing in front of the drain-hole with it, like a man about to drive a stake. And Iason—

Iason was not beside her.

She saw him through the smoke, going round the edge of the pool. Not toward the dead. Toward the man. The quaestor had his back to the crystal now, by the stool, with one hand on the ledger and the other raised, still speaking softly to the dead, directing them as a man directs dogs, and he did not see the apprentice of Cyrene coming round the pool's rim in the steam with a condemned sword of the Fourteenth in his hand.

"*Iason!*" Alana shouted. "*Iason, alive! I need him alive! He knows—*"

The quaestor turned his head at her shout. He saw Iason. He opened his mouth to say a word.

Iason put the sword through his throat.

He did it without a sound, without any art at all, the way a man in a shipyard puts an adze through a plank. The quaestor of the Fourth Survey sat down on his own stool, slowly, with both his hands at his neck and his reading lenses swinging on their cord, and looked up at the young man of Cyrene with an expression of great surprise. Then he fell sideways off the stool onto the flagstones and lay there, and his blood ran across the stones and into the steaming pool, and drifted there, red in the clear blue water, in long slow threads like ink.

And the dead of Cyrene's garrison stopped.

Not all at once. They faltered, the way a line of men falters when its officer goes down. The front ones stood still where they were with their swords half raised. The ones behind walked into them and stopped. A sentence with no one to read it, Alana thought. A word with nothing behind it. They stood in the smoke, swaying, with the light of the Heart-Stone going gold and slow across their dead faces, and did nothing.

"Fire," said Gaius, hoarsely. "Lady. Now. Before they remember."

She took one of the jars of oil from round her neck and broke it on the flagstones in front of them, and drew the sign in it with her left hand, small, quick, crooked, a student's scrawl. And spoke the names of the strokes. And touched the rod to it.

The fire went through them like a word through a crowd.

---

Afterward, when it was done, she sat on the edge of the pool with her burned hand in her lap and her feet in the hot water, because she could not stand any longer, and watched the Heart-Stone breathe.

Gaius had gone up the stair with Fingers to find the gate. She had heard them go, and heard, faintly, through the rock, the noise of fighting somewhere above, and then the great slow groan and clank of a windlass, and then a sound that she had heard once before, from the *Clemency*'s afterdeck, at dawn: two hundred men beating their swords on their shields. She had not moved. Somebody had to watch the stone.

Iason sat at the foot of the scaffold with his sword across his knees, as he had sat on the *Clemency*'s bench. He had not wiped the blade. He was looking at the quaestor, who lay by the stool with his blood still drifting out into the pool.

"I'm sorry," Iason said, after a long time. "You wanted him."

"Yes."

"He'd have told you things."

"Yes."

"I'm not sorry," said Iason. "That's what I'm sorry about." He did not look up. "My mother. And my sisters. They were in the house on Rope Street, by the second slip. The healer has a list. She keeps the names. She showed me." His voice did not change at all. "They're on it."

Alana did not say anything. There was nothing in all the libraries of Corvus that she could have said.

After a while she reached out with her good hand and picked up the ledger from where it had fallen beside the stool. It was a fine book, bound in dark red leather with the white closed hand stamped on the cover, and it was nearly full. She opened it at the last written page.

*Cyrene. Survey of the Fourth Year. Stone.* And a column of figures. *Shard 1. Shard 2. Shard 3.* Down to twenty, and the twentieth unfinished, the ink trailing off.

She turned back a page. Another column, longer. *Cyrene. Survey of the Fourth Year. Stock. Rope Street: 4, 2, 6, 1. Netmakers' Row: 3, 3, 5. Hospital of the Vigil, by the Baths: 61.*

No names. Numbers.

She closed the book, and held it on her knees, and sat looking at the slow gold breathing of the island's heart, and the chisel scars down its side, raw and white, like the marks of a whip on a back.

---

**MARCUS**

The courtyard of the fort of Cyrene was full of the dead, and they were all Tharsian.

Not raised. Only dead. Twenty-odd of them, in their red cloaks, sprawled across the cobbles where the gate had opened and the cohort had come through it in a single roaring wedge with Rufo's split shield at its point. They had not expected the gate. Marcus had seen it in their faces, the ones he had seen: men running to the wall-stairs to meet an assault from outside, and turning, and seeing the gate swinging open behind them with an engineer and a lockpick at the windlass, and the thieves of Aeridor pouring in. It had been very short. It had been short the way all such things are short, when the door is open.

His hands were steady. They had been steady for an hour, standing under the wall in the shield-tortoise with the bolts coming down. He had locked them in close, all of them, two hundred men in a single shell of oak and iron with the faded red *XIV* turned outward on every side and on top, like the scales of some great slow animal, and he had stood in the middle of it, in the dark and the stink and the din of the bolts on the shields, and talked. He could not now remember what about. His voice had never stopped. That was the thing, the old centurions said. Your voice must not stop. And not one of them had broken, and only two had died, Sextus and the one called Little Gallo, and he had their names.

Now he stood at the foot of the square tower with the cohort around him and the gate open behind him, and looked up.

The bell-tower door was shut. It was the last door in the fort that was. Behind it, Gaius said, there was a stair, and the bell-chamber at the top, and the garrison's captain, and what was left of his men.

"How many?" said Marcus.

"I counted eleven," said Gaius. He was black from head to foot with soot and wet, and he had a mallet in his hand that he seemed to have forgotten about. "They went up when the gate opened. The captain's with them. Merula. A sensible man. He'll want terms."

"He can want."

Marcus walked up to the door, and struck it with the pommel of his sword, three times.

"*Captain Merula,*" he said. "*I'm Marcus, son of Aurelian. Your quaestor's dead in the cellar, and your stone-cutting's done, and your gate's mine. Come down.*"

There was a long silence. Then a voice, from high up, at a slit in the tower wall. A tired, flat, dry voice.

"Do I have your word, Highness? For my men?"

Marcus did not answer at once.

He turned his head, because there was a sound behind him that he did not understand. It came from the gate. A murmur, and then a shuffling, and then a great many voices all at once, low and rising. He turned and looked.

The town had come up the hill.

They were in the gate, and in the road below the gate, and on the slope of black rock and dead heather below the walls. Hundreds of them. More than he had known were left. Men and women of Cyrene, gaunt and grey and filthy, in nightshirts and aprons and the clothes they had worn for fifteen days in their cellars and their lofts; old women with sticks; men with boathooks and gaff-hooks and gutting knives; children on shoulders. They had come out of the steam-box house and the net-lofts and the cellars when the bell stopped, and followed the cohort up the hill, and they had come into the gate now behind his men and they stood there in a great silent crowd looking past him at the shut door of the tower.

At their head was a big grey-bearded man with the hands of a netmender, and Tam beside him, holding the dead red banner of the eastern port bundled in his arms. And over the big man's shoulder, coiled, heavy, black with tar, there was a rope.

Marcus knew it. Everyone in the fort knew it. There was only one rope in Cyrene that a man would carry up a hill like that.

"From the gallows on the quay," said a quiet voice at his elbow. "They hanged nine men from it the first week. And a woman. For hiding her son." It was the healer, Liora. She had come up the hill with the town.

He had heard of her from Alana in the chapel, in a dozen words. Nobody had told him what she looked like. She stood at his elbow in the ruin of a white silk gown, torn at the shoulder and split at the side, with somebody's leather apron knotted over it as an afterthought and her golden hair loose down her back like a girl's, and she was the most beautiful woman he had ever stood beside, and he had stood beside a great many. There was a bruise on her cheekbone, yellow-green and old. There was blood on her hands to the wrist. He had known the faces of the great houses all his life, at feasts and funerals and dances, and he knew, without knowing how he knew, that this was one of them.

She was looking not at the rope, but at him. She did not look away when he looked back. "There are two thousand people in this town who didn't choose this. Not the fort falling, not the dead in the street, not you. None of it."

She did not raise her voice. She did not have to.

"Whatever you win today," she said, "win it gently."

Marcus looked at the big netmender with the rope. He looked at Tam, with the banner. At the town behind them in the gate, waiting. At Roderic, on the grey mare at the edge of the crowd, whose face gave him nothing at all. At Iason, who had come up out of the cellar with Alana and stood now beside her with his unwiped sword, looking at the tower door with eyes like two holes burned in a sheet.

Up at the slit in the tower wall, Captain Merula's dry voice came again.

"*Highness? Your word?*"

And the whole of Cyrene turned, and looked at Marcus, and waited to hear what kind of man had come back to them out of the sea.



---

# Chapter Eight: Not a Lady

**MARCUS**

"You have my word," Marcus said, "that no man of yours dies today by my hand or my men's. Come down, Captain."

Behind him the town made a sound. Not a word. A long low grinding breath out of six hundred throats, the sound a shingle beach makes when the wave goes back.

The tower door opened.

They came down one at a time, and threw their swords on the cobbles at the foot of the stair, and the cohort took them by the arms and stood them against the tower wall in a row. Eleven. Two of them were wounded. One was the boy from the eastern port, whom Marcus had bound on the roof that morning and who must have slipped his cords and run for the fort; he saw Marcus and went the color of whey. The rest were what such men always were. Middle-aged, most of them. Thick in the waist. Tired. Red cloaks gone brown with fifteen days of smoke. They stood against the wall with their hands at their sides and did not look at the town.

Captain Merula came last.

Marcus had made a picture of him in his head, from the voice at the slit: a hard lean man, a wolf. He was neither. He was a short broad grey man of fifty with a clerk's stoop and a soldier's hands, and a pair of reading lenses hanging on a cord round his neck exactly like the dead quaestor's, as if it were a uniform of some kind. He came down the last step and unbuckled his sword-belt and held it out, scabbard and all, to Marcus. Not to Rufo. To Marcus.

Marcus took it.

"Highness," said Merula. His voice was the voice from the slit, flat and dry. "I'll tell you now, so you don't waste time. I don't know where the King's pilots are, and I don't know when the next Survey ship was due, and I'd not tell you if I did." He glanced past Marcus at the gate, at the town. "You'll want to give me to them. I'd give me to them, in your place."

"I gave my word."

"You gave it to get the door open. Nobody holds a man to that."

"I do," said Marcus.

Merula looked at him for a long moment, through the stoop and the tiredness, with a pair of small, shrewd, colorless eyes. Then something in his face shifted, very slightly. It was not relief. Marcus thought, later, that it was disappointment: the look of a man who has been told that the world is going to be more complicated than he had hoped.

"Then you're a fool, Highness," the captain said quietly, "and they'll hate you for it."

"Probably."

Marcus turned round.

---

The big netmender had come through the cohort to the front. Nobody had stopped him. He stood ten paces off with the gallows rope coiled on his shoulder and his son at his side, and behind him in the gate the people of Cyrene stood packed like herring in a barrel, waiting.

"Orso," said Marcus. "That's your name? Orso Netmender?"

"It is." The man had a voice like a hull dragged over stones. "And that's mine, Highness, at your back. Eleven of them. And the one with the glass on his neck, that's the one who signed the paper. For the quay. For the rope." He did not raise his voice either. Nobody in Cyrene, Marcus was beginning to understand, raised their voice. They had been fifteen days in cellars, listening for footsteps. "My mother's in the steam-box house with her jaw broke. Rope Street's on the healer's list, all of it. The boy there, the young one, he put a torch to the third slip with his own hand. I saw him. We'll have them, if it's all the same to you."

"It isn't," said Marcus.

The breath went through the crowd again.

He looked at them. He made himself look, at the faces in the gate, one after another, the way he had made himself look at the faces of the cohort from the lid of the arms chest. He saw the hunger in them, and the grief, and something worse than either, something old and patient and cold that he recognized from his own face in a basin, three years of mornings. He thought of the ninety-one. He thought of what he would have done, three years ago, to any Tharsian who had been put in front of him with a rope handy, and how glad he would have been, and how little it would have mended.

"I gave this man my word," he said, loudly enough for the gate to hear. "For his men. I gave it so that I wouldn't have to put two hundred more of mine under that wall. That word's the first thing Aeridor has given Cyrene in three years that's worth anything, and I'll not break it in front of you on the first day, because if I do, you'll never believe another one."

Nobody moved.

"Here's what I'll do. They live. All of them. They go down to the slipways in the morning, in irons, and they work. They clear the ash off the slips they burned. They cut the timber. They set the keels. Every day, in the open, under your eyes, from first light to dark. Every one of you who lost a house, or a son, or a mother, can walk down to the slips and stand there and watch them do it, and nobody will stop you, and nobody will tell you to look away." He looked at Orso. "And they'll be building the ships that take this war back to the people who sent them. Let them carry that every day for a year, Orso Netmender. It's heavier than a rope."

The netmender stared at him. His big hands closed and opened on the coil.

"It's not enough," he said hoarsely.

"No," said Marcus. "Nothing is. Not the rope either. I know, Orso. I've tried the rope." He did not know where that came from. It was true. "I've tried everything a man can try to make it enough, for three years. Nothing makes it enough."

There was a long silence in the courtyard of the fort.

"And the rope?" said Orso at last.

"Keep it," said Marcus. "Hang it on your door. If one of them runs, you'll know where it is."

---

The town did not cheer. He had not expected it to.

It went out of the gate slowly, the way the tide goes out of a harbor, in a long grumbling murmur, and some of them spat on the cobbles as they went and some of them would not look at him and one old woman, quite deliberately, walked up to the row of Tharsians against the tower wall and struck the boy from the eastern port across the face with her stick, once, very hard, and walked away again. Nobody stopped her. The boy did not lift a hand.

Orso went last. He stood for a long time with the coil on his shoulder looking at Marcus, and then he turned and went, and Tam went with him, and halfway to the gate the boy looked back over his shoulder. Marcus could not read his face.

"They'll call it weakness," said a voice at his elbow. The healer, Liora, with her bloody wrists. She was watching the town go.
