The *Tally*'s stern lay against the mole a few paces off, her afterdeck sloping toward the stone, her lantern still swinging beside the binnacle. And there was a man at the binnacle. The grey figure.

He was grey all over: long grey-green coat, a face with no color in it at all. He had wrenched the binnacle's brass-bound box off its post and was dragging it up the sloping deck toward the seaward rail, over the deep water of the gap. The pilot's stones. Every shard that ship had steered by. The way through the Grey to the wood.

He got it onto the rail, his whole body under it, his hands spread on the brass to push.

Something came out of the dark and went *thock* into the rail.

Nobody saw it come. It came out of the landward dark, from the headland or the rocks or the sky: an arrow standing in the rail through the back of the grey man's left hand. He did not cry out. The box slid inboard onto the deck and burst its glass lid.

Iason came over the stern in one movement, as a boy goes over a wall, with a shipwright's knife worn thin, and had the grey man by the hair with the blade at his throat before anyone else reached the rail.

"*Alive!*" Alana was running. "*Alive, Iason! I need him alive!*"

The same words. She heard how they had sounded in the cave, with the quaestor's blood going out into the pool like ink.

Iason held.

She saw the knife shake against the pale throat, and his whole arm with it, the crooked broken knuckle whiter than the rest. The face under his hand looked back at him out of two grey eyes as flat as slate, without fear and without anything else, and did not speak, and did not smile.

Iason took the knife away one finger's width. Then two.

"I heard you," he said, without turning his head. His voice was hoarse. "I heard you last time too."

She looked at the arrow. A long pale shaft, and its fletching, in the swinging lantern-light, was green: the green of moss on the north side of a stone.

"Who's got a bow?" Valeria was at the rail beside her with the Farrier, looking out into the dark of the headland. "Rufo. Who has a bow?"

"Nobody, Captain. Not one. Slings and spears."

Valeria looked at the dark a while, and said nothing else. Alana set it aside, somewhere near a strand of wool.

---

They found the pilot under the chart-table in the stern cabin, where the grey man had thrown him when he took the binnacle: a small neat man with a gold ring in one ear, still in his boat-cloak, who said in good isles' speech, "Hirtius. A King's pilot. I am not a soldier, and I will not be made to steer," and held out his wrists for the irons. The marines on the deck threw down their axes. There were not many left to throw.

Marcus found her at the stern rail with the binnacle's shards spilled glowing in the straw at her feet like coals in ash. There was a long dark stain on his sleeve from elbow to wrist, and he did not seem to know it. His hands were quite steady. The hair on the left side of his head still stood up a little.

"You could have killed me," he said.

"I measured."

"You *measured*?"

"There is a difference," said Alana. She was shaking. She had not noticed till now. "There is currently a difference."

For a moment she thought he would laugh, as he had in the surf. Then she saw that he could not; that he was looking at the blood on her mouth and the cut on her cheek the way she had looked at the weed.

"Two of mine," he said at last. "Rufo's counting. And the boy."

"I saw the boy."

"I told him to walk," said Marcus. "I told him to put the lamp down and walk, and not stay to watch."

Down the mole, in the torchlight, Iason was walking away alone toward the water-gate stair, not hurrying.

---

**LIORA**

At midnight the steam-box house was full of red cloaks.

Nine marines of the *Tally* lay on the pallets where the town's hurt had lain, in the same red the garrison had worn; and the steam came down through the roof-vents, yellow in the lamplight, onto them as it had come down onto other red cloaks in this room, at night, before. Liora did not think about that. She had decided, the first night she was free, which things she would think about in this room, and in what order. She thought about the arm in front of her.

Her hands were steady. She noted it, as she would note a pulse.

She asked each man his name before she touched him, as the Vigil taught: a man with a name is a patient, and a man without one is a wound, and wounds are easier. One would not give it, and halfway through cleaning his cut he told her anyway, as though it had come out with the splinter. Opiter.

The one with the hand was Fannius. Hostus had taken the arm above the elbow on the mole, by torchlight, because the black had reached the elbow before the strap was tight. She unwound the dressing and looked for a long time. The flesh at the cut was not like flesh. It was dark and dry and fine-grained, like old wood, and in the wood there were thin black threads going upward, where the strap had stopped them, as roots stop at a stone.

She knew what it was made of. She wrapped it again, carefully. She knew exactly when she would let herself think about it. Not now.

---

The grey man sat by the furnace door between two of the Ford with the arrow still through his hand. He did not move while she cut the shaft and drew it, though the hand went hard as wood under hers. He watched her with his flat grey eyes, as a heron watches shallow water, and his face told her nothing, as a shut door tells nothing.

She set the arrow on the table. Green-fletched.

---

Iason brought the boy in a little after the middle watch.

He carried him in his arms, as a man carries a child up to bed, and laid him on the bench by the door, and did not go away. The boy's face was quite calm. There was a dark hole in his tunic under the breastbone and very little blood. On his ankles, above the boot-tops, was clean linen. Her linen. She had wound it there three days ago, after the prince came down the slip to ask.

Rufo came after with Merula in his chains. They had kept the rest in the pen that night. Merula they had kept on the wall above the water-gate, gagged, between two of the Ford, because the prince would not have him anywhere he could not be watched.

"He asks to look at the boy, healer," said Rufo. "I said I'd ask you."

"Yes," said Liora. Her right hand found her left wrist, and held it, and let it go.

Merula came and stood by the bench. He put on the lenses, slowly, and looked at the boy's face, and then at the linen on his ankles. Then he took the lenses off.

"I saw it from the wall," he said. His voice was flat and dry. "They looked at his feet. A garrison man, with his galls bound in clean linen by a healer of the Vigil. No man of ours gets that." He did not look at her. "They knew what it meant before he'd finished the words."

Liora said nothing.

"Nobody thought to ask me," said Merula.

"Would you have told us?"

"No." He was quiet a moment. "You say names, they tell me. At night. Over the dead."

"Yes."

"Say his."

"I was going to," said Liora.

He nodded, once, and turned, and Rufo took him out with his chain dragging on the stones. Iason did not move from beside the bench.

---

The prince came in near the end of the second watch, to have his arm seen to, because Rufo had told him to and the barber was asleep on his feet.

He sat on the bucket by her table with his back to the door, and she cut the sleeve away from a long shallow cut, elbow to wrist, and began to stitch. His hand lay on the table under hers, open, and it was perfectly still. She had seen that hand every day since the landing, on the slips and in the courtyard, and it had never been still before. So he had killed tonight, close; and when she glanced up at his face she saw that he knew she knew, and that he hated it, and that he would not say so. She did not say so either. She bent to the needle.

It was clean work, and wanted her hands more than her eyes, and her mind went where it always went after a fight, when there were more men than hands: to the sorting. Who would live without her. Who would die whatever she did. Who was hers. The Vigil taught it to its novices with a slate and chalk, and most of them wept the first time. Liora had not wept. She had grown up in a house in the capital where harder sums were done at table, over the fish, by women who never raised their voices; and she did one now, coolly, while the needle went in and out.

Titus was dead at Hollowmere. She had brought his worst wounded south herself. The king had one son left, and here was his arm on her table. A pardoned exile, and by every account a drunk a month ago: the council would say what it said, and councils said a great deal. But a crown has to go somewhere, and there was nowhere else for this one to go.

And a woman of the Vigil at a king's side, with his ear and the use of his seal, could do a great deal of good for a great many. The hospital by the Baths opened again, with a wall round it and men on the wall. A house for the wounded on every island that had a spring, and a ship kept by each to take them off. No more wounded left in their beds for the enemy because nobody with the right to send a galley had thought of them in time. She did not use the other word, even to herself. She did not need to.

She looked at the arm under her needle and named to herself, exactly, what she liked in him. It was the first rule of the work: look at the thing as it is.

His kindness first, because it was the rarer. He had come down to her about that boy's ankles himself, with ash to the knee; he had not sent Felix, and he had not made a speech of it. And on the black beach, the night she had gone into the warm surf and sat down in it with her arms round her knees, because there was nowhere else on the island to put what she was carrying, he had sat forty paces up the sand with his back to her, and had not come nearer, and had not gone away. Near, and not pressing. She had not known a prince could do it.

Then his strength, which was under her hand. The long forearm, hard from the shield and the slips, that did not flinch from the needle. And the stillness, which was a terrible thing, and was also strength, of a kind; a man who could be that still with tonight on him could be still in a council chamber with a knife at his back. She liked his mouth, too, if she was being exact. She was being exact.

The sum was cold. It would not have been, before the still-room. She noted that too, and set it beside the steady hands, and went on stitching.

Halfway down the arm she looked up past his shoulder and saw Alana in the doorway.

She was without her cloak, with the lamplight on her and the snow coming down behind, and dried blood under her nose, and a cut on her cheekbone that wanted closing. She was watching the needle, or the arm, or the bench by the door where the boy lay. Liora could not tell which. Perhaps Alana could not either. Liora bent to the next stitch, and when she looked up again the doorway was empty. Alana had gone away up the slip into the dark.

So she did the second sum. It was shorter.

The first time Liora dressed that burned hand, Alana had not looked at it in a day, and a woman who will not look at her own hand is not planning to need it long. Something had changed her mind since. Liora could name two things that had, and one of them was sitting on her bucket. The other was two hundred thieves, who called her the Chair and had drummed a real one up the fort road for her, and would carve the face off anyone who made her sorry she had sat in it. And a prince pulled two ways between two women, in a fort, at the start of a war, with nowhere to walk a quarrel off: Liora had seen what that did to a column. She had washed what it did.

She could perhaps win. She was not modest about her face; too many people had told her about it. And she would lose more than she won. The cohort. Alana, who had walked down through the town in the dusk to tell her about the vat with her own mouth, and had not softened a word, because she had judged that Liora could bear it; nobody else on the island had paid her that compliment. And the man himself, split down the middle, which was no use to anybody, least of all to a kingdom.

Not hers. Not now.

She tied off the last stitch and bound it, and put the green-fletched arrow in his good hand.

"It came out of the grey man's hand," she said. "It isn't ours."

He turned it over. He did not ask her anything else. He made to rise.

She kept two fingers on his wrist, over the linen, a moment longer than the binding needed, and he stopped, and looked at her.

"I took no vows, Highness," said Liora. "At the Vigil. People forget it, because of the collar." Her voice was low and clear, and did not bend. "I would not have you forget it, if things should change. It isn't a question. You needn't answer it."

He did not answer it. She watched it reach him, and watched him understand it, all of it, the first time; whatever else he had been in the capital, he had not been a fool about women. Something went over his face and was put away, carefully, to be looked at later. Under her two fingers his pulse, as steady all this while as his hand, stumbled once, and went on faster.

She noted it, and let go of his wrist.

At the door he stopped, and she thought he would turn round. He did not. He lifted one hand a little way, as he had on the black sand, and let it fall, and went out and down the slip toward the mole, where there were still men to be seen to.

She had not told him who had stood in the doorway. Alana had not come in; that was hers to tell, or not.

It was snowing harder. Liora sent Kaeso up the slip after her with a blanket.

---

Before dawn, when the shed was quiet, she stood by the bench at the door with her hands folded and said them. Low, in the old vowels, as the priests of the Sun say the litany on Midwinter night: the names she had said every night since the Baths, all eighty, and the town's, street by street; and then the new ones, at the end.

"Nonius, of Tharsis. Fourteen; fifteen at the thaw. Who held the lamp.

"Crispus, of the cohort, who was a cutpurse, and was killed on the mole by a bolt.

"Mettius, of the cohort, who ran from a war once and did not run from this one.

"Larcius, of the *Tally*, who asked me for water and was dead before I brought it.

"And the others of the *Tally*, whose names nobody asked."

Iason, beside her, did not say anything. But when she had finished he bent and straightened the linen on the boy's ankles, which had come loose, and tucked the ends in, the way she would have done it.

---

**MARCUS**

They warped the *Tally* off the mole on the morning flood and laid her alongside the water-gate stair, where she had meant to lie.

Nerva walked her. He went the length of her deck in the grey light with one hand on her rail, as a man walks round his wife the first morning home from a long war, touching things, saying something to her now and then under his breath. At the stern Pell stood at a tiller lashed to nothing, grinning, and Gaius was half over the taffrail with his lenses down, measuring her rudder-stock. "Proper iron this time," he said, to nobody.

Hirtius sat on the main hatch in irons, looking at the sea as if he resented it. The grey man had gone ashore at first light between two of the Ford, without a word.

Alana knelt by the binnacle box in the blanket Kaeso had brought her, moving the shards one at a time in their straw and watching each turn in its bed to point.

"This one," she said, and lifted it. It was not like the others. They were honey-gold, like the King's Shard; this was gold with green in it, the green of a leaf held up to the sun, cut in long narrow planes that met at the point like the old work in the Corvus drawings. It lay in her palm and turned, and pulled, steadily, toward the west: toward the edge of the old charts, on a lane no pilot of Aeridor had steered in years.

"Silverwood," said Alana. "It's warm."

Valeria, come up the stair behind them, looked over Alana's shoulder, and Marcus saw her face change.

"That's a crown stone," said Valeria.

Nobody asked her what she meant. Alana looked up at her, and then at Marcus, and closed her hand over the shard, carefully, as a woman closes her hand over a coal she has decided to carry.

---

He had the arrow in his fingers. He had asked every man on the mole. Atticus had looked at the fletching a long time and said in his slow voice, "Not ours, Centurion. Nobody of mine could make that shot in the dark. I'd like to meet who could." Rufo said nobody. The Ford said nobody. Roderic said, "Somebody who wanted that box more than the grey one did," and limped off to see to the chestnut mare.

Nobody claimed it. He put it inside his tunic.

"She'll want a new name," said Nerva, beside him at the rail. "I'll not sail a ship called after a clerk's sum."

Marcus thought of a grey dawn off the Kettle Bank, and two hundred men on the *Clemency*'s deck, before she was a wreck, shouting one word.

"*Forward*," he said.

Nerva tried it in his mouth, as he tasted the lead. Then he nodded, and went to tell her.

---

He took out the oilskin.

The ninety-one, in their three columns, in the order they had stood on the stair. His father's letter with the black bar through it. And the page from the back of Alana's weather-book, with seven names on it, four in her hand and three in his. He took the pen from the pilot's cabin, a Tharsian pen, and wrote two more.

*Crispus, cutpurse. Mettius, deserter.*

That made nine. Two hundred and three.

Then he left a space, and at the very bottom of the page, by itself, he wrote one more. *Nonius.* He had no right to put him among the others, and he could not leave him off. So he put him apart, where he could see him.

His hands had begun to shake again, now that there was nobody left to fight. He held the page flat on the rail until the ink took.

Roderic came back and stood at the rail beside him, leaning on his forearms to spare his back, and looked down at the bottom step where the lamp had been. His face was grey with no sleep, and there was a horse's slobber on his sleeve. Marcus felt the question come up in him, as it always did now when the old man stood beside him with nothing to be done. *Whose order was it?*

There were twelve flasks under the hill, and a pilot in irons, and a stone in Alana's fist that pointed at a dying wood somewhere west through the Grey. There was a boy on a bench in the steam-box house with linen on his ankles.

It was not a day they could spare.

He folded the page and put it back against his ribs, with the ninety-one and the letter and the arrow, and went down the stair to see who else was hurt.

---

# Chapter Twelve: Four in Five

**MARCUS**

They burned the dead at noon on the black beach, on one fire.

There had been some talk of two. Marcus had not let it go far. So Crispus the cutpurse went onto the stacked pine with Mettius, who had run from a war once, and beside them the marines of the *Tally* whose names Liora had and the ones whose names she had not; and in the middle, because that was where Iason laid him, the boy who had held the lamp. Liora said the litany. Kaeso beat the slow stroke. The pine came out of the third yard and burned hot and fast and sweet, and by the middle of the afternoon the whole town smelled of it.

He went down to the slips afterward because he did not know where else to go.

The ten were on the third slip, setting blocks for a new keel where the ash had been. Rufo sat on his bollard above them with his split shield against his knee. Iason sat on his, ten paces off, with his sword across his knees, as he had sat every day since the fifth night; only there was nobody now at the end of his ten paces, and he kept his eyes on the place where the boy had worked, as if the boy might come back to it.

Captain Merula straightened when Marcus came down the slip, and put his hands in the small of his back, and looked at him through the lenses on their cord.

"You put him on the fire with your own," he said.

"There was one fire."

"I saw it from the pen." The voice was as flat as ever. "At home we don't burn them, Highness. A garrison boy who died like that, on a stair, at night. They'd have had him up by the next noon. Carrying something." He considered it. "Stone, probably. They're always short of men for stone."

Marcus said nothing. There was nothing in him to say it with.

Merula set his shoulder to a block, and two of his men took the other end, and they walked it a pace down the slip with their chains dragging and set it in its bed. He came back wiping his hands on a red cloak gone brown from shoulder to hem with a fortnight of ash and steam.

"You've had the engineer over her stern since dawn," he said. "Measuring for proper iron. Men don't put proper iron on a ship they mean to leave in harbor. You'll be going west. With the iron."

"You can't know that."

"I've added columns for the Survey four years, Highness. You learn to see the sum before it's written." He took off the lenses and wiped them. "I'll tell you something, for nothing. Not because you kept your word, though you did, and I said you were a fool for it, and you are." He looked down the slip at the empty place. "Because he'd have told you, and he's not here to."

Marcus waited.

"The King won't let go of the wood. He's written it down. I've never known him strike a thing out of his books. Not an island, not a man, not three coppers owed to a chandler in Vessa." The colorless eyes did not move off his face. "You'll think you've ruled that column off. You've taken his ship and his pilot and his twelve. You haven't. It's a column he hasn't finished adding. He'll send another ship. And when the ships stop, he'll send something worse, and it won't be a ship."

"And the five on the island."

"Survey men." Merula said it the way a farmer says *blight*. "I've had their kind through my gate four years. They don't drink. They don't dice. They count." He bent for his shovel. "Five men like that, with iron enough to last, and no ship coming to fetch them home. They'll not sit under a tree and wait. They'll finish the job with what they've got. And they'll do it faster, because nobody's coming to tell them to stop."

He drove the shovel into the ash at the slip's edge.

"I'd not like to see it wasted," he said to the shovel. "What you did at the pen, the fifth night. A man doesn't see it often. I'd not like to see it go into the ground on some island for want of being told."

"Thank you, Captain."

"Don't. I'm a prisoner. You'll make the others think I've gone soft." He did not look up again. "And tell your healer the boy's ankles were well done. I never said."

On his bollard, Iason had turned his head. He looked at the grey captain a long time. Then he looked away, out at the harbor, where the *Tally* lay against the water-gate stair with her new name not yet painted on her, and Nerva's white braid going up and down her deck like a needle through sailcloth.

---

They held the council in the cave, because it was warm, and because it was the only room in Cyrene big enough that had no door onto the street.

Felix had carried down a table from the hall, and stools, and a jug of the spring's water that nobody touched, and a dish of the Tharsian figs, which Roderic ate steadily, one after another, as a man feeds a fire he means to keep in all night. The Heart-Stone breathed over all of them. Its slow gold came and went across their faces, honey and shadow and honey again, and the chisel scars down its flank stood out raw in every brightening.

They were nine, and two. Marcus. Roderic, with his back against a pillar. Valeria, upright on her stool as if the stool were on parade, her bill leaned on the rock behind her. Alana at the pool's rim with her bandaged hand in her lap and a fresh cut down her cheekbone from the west tower, closed with three of Hostus's small disapproving stitches. Gaius beside her with his tablet. Liora, a little apart, her face still not the color it had been before the still-room. Rufo, standing, because he would not sit in front of officers whatever anyone said. Nerva on his heels by the water like a man waiting on a tide. And Orso Netmender, whom Marcus had sent for, on the very edge of his stool with his big hands on his knees, looking at the Heart-Stone of his own island for the first time in his life, as a man looks at the sea from a ship he does not trust.

Felix moved among them with the jug. Kaeso sat on the bottom step of the stair by the door with his drum between his knees, because he went where the drum went, and the drum went where Marcus went, and nobody had ever told him otherwise.

On the warm rim of the pool, in a row on a strip of linen, lay the stones.

---

"Nineteen of ours," said Alana. "Cyrene's. They point at this." Every one lay with its face to the Heart-Stone, like a row of hounds at a kitchen door. "They'll take us home. That's all they'll ever do. Now these."

She laid out the binnacle's stones beside them from the brass-bound box with the burst lid. Eight. Seven of them honey-gold like Cyrene's own, and no two the same gold: one pale as new straw, one dark as old ale, one with a smoke in it like a flaw in glass. Hirtius would not say what any of them were. He would not steer and he would not speak.

"It doesn't matter. He wrote it down." She unrolled a chart across the table: Tharsian vellum, the coasts in black and the lanes in red ink thin as a hair and perfectly straight. There were more red lanes on it than Marcus had ever seen on any chart in his father's house. They went across the Grey from island to island like a spider's work across a doorway. "Every lane he's sailed, and the stones he sailed it by." She touched a stone of dull gold. "This one is Corvus."

It lay pointing north, a little west of north, toward a burned library and a harbor where the dead had stood in rows for a week. Her fingers rested on it a moment longer than they needed to. Then she took them away.

"And this." The green-gold one, the crown stone, cut in its long narrow planes. It paid Cyrene's heart no more mind than the Corvus stone did. It lay with its point to the west and pulled, as if there were a thread on it. "Silverwood."

She set three stones on the chart at Cyrene and drew the lines with her finger.

"One stone tells you which way. It won't tell you where you are, or how far. Two from two islands, and you can hold the line between them, as a ropewalker holds a rope between two posts. Three, and you'll know when you've slid off it." Her finger went out west along a red lane into the white of the Grey. "Silverwood on the bow. Cyrene astern. Corvus on the right hand, pulling north. When Corvus draws aft of the beam, you're past halfway. The pilot's log has the passages. Three days out, against the set. Less, coming home."

"Three," said Nerva from the water, "if the stone's honest and the Grey's in a good temper." He spat into the pool, thought better of it, and looked apologetically at the Heart-Stone. "She never is."

"Three days," said Marcus.

"The first of them's ours," said Valeria.

Everyone looked at her.

"A day in Cyrene's Grey, if Nerva's right. Then the seam." She had not moved her hands from her knees. "The waters of an island end where its Grey ends. Every pilot knows it, and so does every magistrate. At the seam, Highness, the writ ends. And so does everything in it."

---

"You read it on the quay, Captain," said Marcus. "I know what it says."

"I'm saying it so it's been said in this room, by me, before anyone decides anything." It was the drill-field voice, each word set down by itself. "Your pardon's good. They can't take that back without making the king a liar. But the cohort's sentences were suspended, not pardoned. At the seam, every man who goes with you is what he was the morning they put him on the *Clemency*. Condemned. On the books."

"Begging your pardon, Captain." Rufo's face had gone the color of the oak of his shield. "If the men sail, it comes back? All of it?"

"All of it. I'll not lie to you, optio."

Rufo looked at Marcus.

"Then the men ought to hear it, sir. From you. They'll ask me what was said down here, and I'll get it wrong, and they'll believe the wrong of it." He drew a breath. "They're on the stair, sir. Some of them. I told them to go away. They went up three steps."

Marcus looked past Kaeso, up the stair that climbed out of the gold light into the dark of the fort. He had thought it was the dark. The dark had faces in it.

"Then let them come down," he said. "As far as they like. It's their necks."

Kaeso said something up the stair, and the faces came down, one step, five, ten, until the stair was full of men from the gold to the dark, the cohort and the Ford together, packed like staves in a cask and not making a sound.

"There's another thing," said Gaius, not looking up from his tablet. "Since we're saying things. One hull on the slips I can save. Two more I can build, with the oak in the third yard and the town's men and the ten. Three ships by the spring. And not one mast between them. There's no mast timber on Cyrene. There never was." He looked up. "The best mast pine in the isles grows on Silverwood. A hundred feet without a knot. Your grandfather's fleet was masted from it, Highness. I've seen the old yard-books. If we're going there anyway."

"We'll come to that," said Marcus.

"It's a sum. I don't make them. I only add them."

---

Roderic set down the last fig.

He wiped his fingers on his beard, and leaned his head back against the pillar, and looked up at the Heart-Stone as if it were a sky he was reading for weather.

"I'll say the thing nobody's said." He never needed to raise his voice. "We go to Silverwood on a Tharsian ship that's been calling at their shore since the end of summer with poison in her hold. They'll know her sails and her lines and the stink of her. They'll have watched her come in at every dark of the moon with iron for the five men kneeling at their roots." He turned his head on the pillar. "And we'll come in on her, with twelve more of those flasks in her hold. We walk into their wood carrying their poison, boy."

"Yes," said Marcus. "And put it in their hands."

Roderic looked at him a long moment.

"Crowns and crows," he said softly, to the Heart-Stone. "He means it."

"They'll shoot us," said Valeria, "before we've a foot on their beach."

"Some of them will want to," said Marcus. "I would."

"I go with the flasks." Liora had not raised her voice either; nobody in this room did. It was the town's habit, and it had got into all of them. "I said it on the floor of that room, and the Chair said yes. Wherever they go, I go. If the elves shoot, they'll shoot a woman of the Vigil carrying the sixty-one who were steeped to make it. I'll say the names to them first. All eighty. They can shoot after."

Nobody tried to fill the silence after that.

And into it, at last, Orso Netmender said, "Don't."

---

He had got up off his stool. He stood at the end of the table with his netmender's hands hanging at his sides and the gold going over his face and away, and he did not look at the stone now, or the chart, or the stair. He looked at Marcus.

"Don't leave us," he said.

It was the hull dragged over stones, as it had been in the courtyard with the rope on his shoulder. It was not loud.

"You came out of the sea, Highness. Fourteen days since. The Tharsians came out of the sea too, before you. And before them the king's men came out of it, and went back into it, and we were told they'd return, and they didn't. That's what the sea is, here. Everybody comes out of it. Everybody goes back." His hands closed and opened, as they had on the coil. "I've a rope on my door, on a nail. Every morning I come out and look at it and go on, because you asked me to. Nobody's touched it. And now you're taking the ship, and the men, and yourself, to save some other island's trees. And when the next ship comes in through that gap at the dark of the moon, who'll be on the stair?"

Nobody answered him.

Marcus sat and looked at the netmender, and thought of a fisher-boy on the weed-rocks under the eastern port, barefoot, with a gutting knife; and of a red banner coming down off a pole; and of what he had said to the boy at the foot of it. *It's yours. You only let us help.* He had said it because it was true, and because the boy needed something to hold. He had not, until this moment, understood what he had promised.

He stood up.

---

"Kaeso," he said. "Bring the drum."

The boy came across the flagstones with the drum held against his chest in both arms, as Tam had held the banner, and set it on the table between the chart and the jug. An old legion side-drum with the paint gone off it and the head patched with sheep's hide.

"Sit down, Orso. Please."

The netmender looked at him, and sat.

"Prefect of Cyrene and its waters," said Marcus. "To hold them for the crown. I've been thinking since the quay what that means. Hold them how. There are two ways I know. The way the Tharsians held them, with a gallows on the quay and a clerk counting the doors. And an older way." He laid his hand flat on the chart, on Cyrene. "Here's how I'll hold it.

"From today, four parts of Cyrene in five belong to the people of Cyrene, and to nobody else, after I'm gone and after my father's gone. Four fields in five, up on the hill. Four boats in five, and the fishing grounds out to the edge of your Grey. Four seats in five at whatever council this town wants to sit, and it'll sit it, Orso, not me. Choose who you like. Choose the old woman with the stick. And four spears in five on this wall, from the day you've men to carry them, will be Cyrene's own men, from Cyrene's own streets. Not mine. Yours."

Orso did not move.

"The crown keeps the fifth part. One harbor: the gap and the moles and the four ports, so that a king's ship can always come in. One road, from the quay to this fort. One seat at your council, to listen, and be outvoted four times in five." He put his fingers on the drum. "And this. The right to beat the drum when the isles are in danger. When it beats, Cyrene comes. Every island comes. That's the bargain." He looked down at the boy. "Once, Kaeso."

Kaeso took up a stick and struck the drum once.

It went off the water and the rock and the Heart-Stone and came back from every side of the cave, and up the stair through all those silent men, and away into the fort above. Marcus felt it in the soles of his feet.

"That's all a king's ever owned of Cyrene, by rights," he said. "A harbor and a road and a drum. The rest was always yours. We let you forget it. We forgot it first."

Orso stared at him. His mouth worked.

"And the fifth seat," he said at last, hoarse. "Who sits it, while you're gone west?"

"Whoever holds the harbor for the crown while I'm gone. The men I leave, suspended and on the king's books and doing exactly what the writ says. I'll name him before I sail."

Something moved in the netmender's face. It was not a smile. It was the place where a smile had been, before the cellars.

"It's more than we asked," he said.

"It's what you were owed. That's not the same."

---

"That's not in any writ," said Valeria.

The white seam down her face had drawn tight, as it did in the cold.

"No," said Marcus. "It's older than writs."

"Then I'd like to hear it. Since I'm to report everything."

"You'll hear it." He looked up the stair at the faces, thieves and debtors and the men who had held the causeway, all looking down into the gold. "You'll all hear it. It's the only thing I've got to tell you that I didn't learn in a tavern."

He did not get up on anything. He found he did not want to.

"Six islands in four years. You know the count. And every island we lose, we lose all of it. Not the land only. The men. The Fourteenth's wounded were killed in their beds in this town. Nineteen of them walked out under the sea beside Cyrene's fishwives to keep the Kettle Bank against us, and the rest went into a vat. Titus's own dead came up out of the river at Hollowmere in their blue cloaks and walked into the Fourteenth. You've seen the quaestor's book. *Stock.* That's what we are to him. Every man of ours he kills is one more of his. Every woman. Every boy on a stair with a lamp." His hands shook on the edge of the table, and he let them. "We cannot out-number a king who counts our dead as stock. Not if every man in Aeridor picked up a spear tomorrow. The more of us he kills, the more he has. Gaius, tell me I've added it wrong."

"You haven't," said Gaius.

"So I'm not going to try. I'm going to do what my house did once, before it forgot how."

He took his hand off the drum.

"Aeridor didn't begin with a crown. It began with a ship. A ship's master called Aerid, from a rock nobody wanted, sailed the Grey from island to island with no stone in his binnacle but his own, and at every landing he got down out of his boat and asked for one thing: a shard of their Heart-Stone, freely given, so his ship could find them again when they needed him. For every shard he gave a promise back, four parts in five of your own island forever, and the crown to keep only a harbor, a road and a drum. They called him the Binder. He never once took a stone; he carried a box of shards that other people had put in his hands, and that box was the whole of the kingdom."

He glanced, without meaning to, at the green-gold stone under Alana's hand. Then away.

"We were never the strongest people in the isles. The elves had their wood before we had a word for one, and the dwarves had better steel, and the fen-clans had held their water since before there were keels. We were the ones who kept our word. That was the only thing Aeridor ever had that nobody else did.

"And then we stopped."

It was harder than the rest. He had known it would be.

"My grandfather sent Silverwood a treaty in the spring. In the autumn he sent the axes, and cut the pine on their western shore for a war-fleet, and called it the crown's part. It was never the crown's part. My father's council had letters from Stoneheart two famine winters running, asking for grain, and answered them with apologies on very good paper. I was at that table, the second winter. I was drunk. I thought it was funny how long the letters were." Somebody on the stair drew a breath. "The fens asked for a border with their own name on it, drawn by their own hand, and nobody ever drew it. And our banners went through the villages on the edges of the old lands, on the way to somewhere that mattered, and took bread and roofs and firewood, and never looked down to see what they'd passed through." He stopped. "That's my house. That's my name. I carry it with the ninety-one, and it's heavier, because the ninety-one weren't all my fault, and this was."

He looked at Roderic. The old man was looking at the floor.

"So I'm not going to Silverwood to offer them anything. A man who's broken his word doesn't get to come back with a new one in his fist and ask to be believed. I'm going to give them back what was done to them. Twelve flasks, and the day-book, and the ship, and the woman who said the names of the eighty over the vat. And then I'm going to stand on their ground and let them decide what I am." He spread his shaking hands. "That's the whole plan. It's a poor plan. It's the only one I've got that doesn't end with every man in this cave standing up again after he's dead, carrying stone for Lucan."

---

"And if they shoot?" said Roderic, without lifting his eyes.

"Then here's the rule. It's the only order I'll give in this cave. Everything else I'll ask.

"Kill the Tharsians freely. Kill no elf. Not one. Not if they shoot first. Not if they shoot me."

The stair moved. It was not a sound; it was a shifting, as a field of barley shifts when the wind goes over it.

"Highness," said Valeria, very quietly. "I command men who are going to be shot at."

"Behind your shields. As long as it takes. I've seen your line stand still in front of a horse, Captain. I've seen it take an old fool off a horse twice without breaking one of his bones, and the third time the horse had more sense than he did. If any line in the isles can stand under arrows without loosing back, it's yours." He held her eyes. "And if they kill me, they'll have killed a prince of the house that cut their trees, and nobody of mine raised a hand. Let them sing that. It'll be the first true song about my family in sixty years."

Valeria did not answer.

"And the masts," said Gaius.

"No."

"Highness, it's a sum—"

"I know what it comes to. Three hulls and no masts. I'll find you masts if I have to pull them out of the sea. Not there. If one tree on that island falls for our ships," said Marcus, "we're my grandfather. We may as well cut our own throats on their beach and save them the arrows."

The engineer looked at him over the lenses. Then he pushed them up onto his forehead, slowly, and drew a single line through something on his tablet.

"Then I won't cut them," he said. "I want to measure them."

"You've not met an elf," said Nerva.

"Neither have you."

"No," Nerva agreed. "But I've met a great many trees."

---

Marcus turned to the stair.

"Now the asking. And it is asking. I'll not have it said afterward that any man here was ordered.

"The writ is good in Cyrene. Every man who stays keeps his suspension and his name, and does what the king sent him to do. He holds Cyrene: the harbor and the drum, for a town that's just been given back four parts of itself and needs men on its wall while it learns to carry them. Nobody, not me, not the captain's report, not the songs, will call that anything but what it is. Ask the Ford. Holding is the hardest thing there is. I'll knock down the man who calls it shame.

"Every man who comes with me breaks the writ with me. At the seam he's condemned again, as he was on the *Clemency*'s deck, and I can't promise him it'll be lifted. I've no ships and no gold and no writ for it. I've a word. You've seen what I spend it on."

The Heart-Stone breathed in and out across their faces.

"Aerid didn't take the stones," he said. "He asked. And they came down to the beach and put them in his hands. That's the only way it's ever worked. So I'm asking. Anyone who wants to come, come down."

For a moment nobody moved.

Then Decimus Rufo picked up his split shield from where it leaned against the table's leg, and put it on his arm, and walked the six paces to the pool's edge, and stood there.

"I've been condemned longer than I've been anything else, sir," he said. "I know how it sits."

Atticus came down the stair, slowly, through the packed men, in no more hurry than the weather, and stood by Rufo with his sling round his waist; and behind him, without anyone telling them, came his eleven. Iason came down. Marcus had not known he was on the stair. He stood a little apart, looking at the green-gold stone on the chart.

Then the stair broke, the way the ice had broken off the *Clemency*'s bows north of the Grey: in one piece and then another and then all together, men coming down into the gold light one after another and standing by the pool with their faces turned to him. He saw Oppius start down, and the baker's one eye go to Orso, and to the town's cold ovens, and the baker stop on the bottom step and stay there with his face working, which was harder, and was holding. He saw Fingers come halfway and look back up into the dark and stop. He saw Old Baebius not come at all, but stand at the top of the stair against the light with his bill on his shoulder, looking down at his captain.

Kaeso did not come down. He had been down all along. He only stood up.

There were far more of them by the pool than the *Forward* could carry. Marcus looked at them and could not speak. Ninety-one men had stood on a stair for him once, and nobody had asked them.

"Rufo," he said at last. "Forty."

"The ones who won't put it down, sir. I know them."

Felix looked at the men by the pool, and at the stair, and at the old drum on the table, and set down the jug.

"I'll pack for a ship, then," he said.

---

Valeria had not moved. She sat with her hands on her knees and looked at the men by the pool as she had looked at the towers coming out of the fog: counting. Then she looked at Marcus.

"The *Patience* sails for home on the morning tide, as she was always going to," she said. "She'll carry my report. I'll write that the Prefect of Cyrene gave four parts of a crown town to the people living in it, on his own word, in a cave. I'll write that he took a captured ship beyond Cyrene's waters without the council's leave, with a cohort whose sentences that act restores." She did not soften any of it. "And I'll write: *He sailed without leave. I went with him.*" She stood, and took the Farrier off the rock. "Twenty of the Ford, and me. Baebius has the forty, and the harbor, and the fifth seat. He'll vote against the town on principle. He's never let go of anything in his life, including a grudge against me for the shields."

"Captain. Varro's orders. If you go, you've broken them."

"I've read them again. Three times, in the night, by a bad lamp." She put the bill on her shoulder. "Hold Cyrene: Baebius has it. Report everything: I will. Don't let you sail without leave: I've failed, and I'll say so, and they can have my captaincy for it. If you drink, bring you home." Her eyes went, very briefly, to the jug nobody had touched. "You haven't."

"Then—"

"There's one more. Nobody wrote it down. Every captain who's ever been sent after a king's son knows it without being told." She turned for the stair, and the gold light came and went over the white line of the scar. "My orders say keep you alive, Highness. They don't say where."

---

**LIORA**

They loaded the *Forward* at the water-gate stair in the last of the light, at the top of the flood.

The cohort carried its own kit. The casks and the stores Rufo gave to the prisoners, and gave his reasons to nobody in particular, looking down at the weed on the lower steps, green-black and shining. "If any man's going to break his neck on that," he said, "it's not going to be a thief." So the ten came out of the water-gate one behind another with casks on their shoulders and a stride of chain between each man's ankles, and the chains rang on every step going down, and they climbed again empty.

Liora stood on the landing halfway, where the stair turned back on itself against the rock, with her satchels at her feet, sorting what went with her from what stayed with Hostus. She was nearly done. She had been careful all day to be nearly done with something.

She had not set foot on this stair since the fort fell. She had made sure of it.

She had not been on it that night either. They had kept her up in the fort, between two red cloaks, at a table of their own wounded, and in the morning the beds at the Baths were empty. She had never seen it done. She had seen it every night since, all the same, as you see a thing you have been told and not shown: eighty, down these steps in their shirts and their bandages, in no hurry at all, and the sea taking each of them at the bottom, the head last, so that after each one there was a ring on the water, and then not. She had counted them in, in the dark, more times than there were of them. Nobody had counted them out. Four days ago, in the steam-box house, Alana of Corvus had told her where the count went after that.

From the landing she could see the sea-door, round its fold of rock at the waterline beside the stair's foot: low, iron, on its new Tharsian hinges, shut. The two crates had gone out through it first, before the light began to go, slung from its sill straight into the *Forward*'s hold, touching nothing that grew. The sixty-one lay under the straw in the hold now, in twelve flasks of iron, and in the morning she would sail on top of them. She had asked to.

The big one had the lead.

It was Atticus's sling-lead, in a squat iron-hooped keg no bigger than a milk-pail that weighed what a boy weighs, and Rufo had given it to the biggest man in the line because that was arithmetic. He carried it high on his left shoulder with his left arm over it and his right hand free. His red face was redder at the bottom of the stair than at the top.

His name was Statius. She knew it because she had asked it.

The day after the captain of the Ford put her hook round his neck on the slip, the prince had come to the door of the steam-box house about Nonius's galled ankles; and then, in the voice he used when he expected to be refused, about the big man's back. She had said yes to both. Rufo brought them down in their chains and stood by the door, and she asked the big man his name before she touched him, as the Vigil taught, and he gave it to the floor. He had been one of the two in the courtyard who dropped their eyes.

There had been three. She had known which three when they stood against the tower wall on the first day, and before the town was out of the gate she had decided that she would never say which, or what. Not to the prince. Not to Alana. Not to the Sun on Midwinter night. She did not say it now, even inside her own head. It was hers.

She had laid her hands on his back, and they had been perfectly steady, and she had noted it. Below the last rib the long muscles either side of the spine had drawn up into knots the size of a child's fist, and they drew tighter with every load, until about each quarter of an hour he had to straighten and put his hands there and let them go. If a weight came on him with his body turned before he had let them go, they would seize altogether and pinch the great cord that runs down the back of the leg; and the leg would go from under him, and nothing he could do would stop it. She told him so. *Straighten before it comes. With a load on you, turn your feet, never your back.* He said, *Yes, healer,* to the floor, and Rufo took him back to the pen.

She had not thought about him since, except in the order she had decided. She thought about him now because he was on the stair, and she was counting. She counted everything on a stair. He straightened at the top, in the water-gate, every third trip, with both hands pressed to his back below the ribs and his face turned up to the yellow Grey. She saw him do it twice before she knew she was watching. Rufo had been nearly right. It was a little under the quarter.

On his next trip down, Nerva stopped the line.

"Nothing more aboard," he said from the waist, not loudly, "till Pell's trimmed her. She's down by the head. I'll not sail a pig."

So the line stopped where it stood, every man under his load. Statius was at the bottom. He stood on the last step the tide had left them, at the lip, where the stair had no rail and never had: the brow to the ship on his right hand, and on his left nothing but deep water going down green-black past the sea-door. Merula stood one step above him with a water-cask. Old Baebius of the Ford leaned on his bill on the step below the landing.

He had straightened at the top two trips ago. He should have been climbing empty now, toward the place where he eased it. Instead he stood on the weed with the lead on his shoulder, waiting on Pell.

She watched him as she watched a man on a pallet who has not yet told her where it hurts. He shifted his weight from one foot to the other, and the chain clinked. His free hand went round to the small of his back and pressed, and came away, and went back to the keg. Under the red, his face was going the color of tallow, and wet. She had seen that on a hundred faces. A little more. A little longer.

She saw all of it at once: the weed, the lip, the lead, the chain, the water, the count. And she saw that nobody else on the stair could see it, because nobody else on the stair had laid hands on that back.

In her apron her hand was closed round a little pot of liniment, camphor in hog's lard. She had made it up that morning, one for every hurt she was leaving behind her, and his had been one of them. She did not leave a hurt untended. That was simply true, and it was still true.

She counted three.

"Statius," she said.

Not loudly: the voice she used down the length of the steam-box house to a man on the farthest pallet, which carried without being raised.

He turned. Of course he turned. He turned his head and his shoulders up the stair toward her, under the lead, with his feet where they were.

She saw it go into his face.

The leg went all at once, as she had told him it would. The keg took him to the left, and there was nothing on the left but the edge. His free hand went out flat on the weed and slid. The chain went over the lip after him with a long dragging ring, and the sea took him whole, the head with the rest, and the red cloak spread once on the black water and went down after him. The lead and the iron took him straight down past the sea-door.

A few bubbles came up. Then fewer.

Nobody moved.

That was not quite true. Everybody went on with what they had been doing. The Ford man on the landing beside her looked out at the Grey with great interest, as if someone had asked him its color. In the water-gate Rufo made a mark on his tally-stick, and examined it. On the rock above the stair Atticus's eleven sat on their packs and went on eating pickled fish. Pell, half out of the forehatch, found something in the coaming that needed his whole attention. Nerva coiled down a rope that was coiled already. At the stern Gaius had hung his lantern on the rudder-head, and he did not look up from the pintle at all; his mallet went *tap*, and a pause, and *tap*. Of the three women of the town at the head of the stair, one unfolded her arms and folded them the other way. Iason, on his step ten paces from the line with his wiped sword across his knees, watched the bubbles until they stopped. The nine on the stair stood under their loads and looked at their feet.

Merula, on the step above the empty one, set his cask down on the weed. He took off his lenses and wiped them slowly on the brown cloak, and put them back on. Then he bent and lifted the cask again, and stood with it, and waited for the line to move.

Liora heard herself say what a healer says. "Get him out." Clearly, at the proper loudness.

"Healer," said Rufo agreeably, and made another mark.

"Man in the water," observed Old Baebius. It was the voice he used for rain.

"So there is," said Rufo.

"Nerva," said Pell, from the hatch. "Your hook."

"Not the ash one."

"It's a man."

"It's a Tharsian," said Nerva. "That's good ash. Thirty years I've had that hook, and I'll not have it fouled." He went on coiling. "There's the old one. Forward. Under something."

Pell climbed out of the hatch, not quickly, and went forward to look under things.

Old Baebius sighed, and came down to the lip, and leaned out over it with his bill, and fished. He fished slowly and with method. Presently he hooked something and drew it up hand over hand along the haft, and it was the cloak. It came up alone, streaming, torn out at the shoulder where the pin had been, the wool gone rotten with ash and steam. Baebius held it up dripping on the hook and considered it.

"Tharsian weave," he said, with contempt. He did not let go of it. He had never let go of anything in his life; his captain had said so in the cave that afternoon.

Rufo sent one of his shields up to the fort for a longer pole. The man went at a great pace and did not come back for a good while; she could hear him at the head of the stair, telling the women of the town all about it.

Fingers was on the rock among the slingers. He was not sailing, and had come down to see his customers off. He was offering evens that the man came up before full dark, and fifty to one that he came up breathing, in a low and reasonable voice, and finding no takers at either price.

At the stern Gaius let a line down past the rudder with a pintle-bolt on the end of it, and drew it up, and measured it off along his arm, and wrote on his tablet.

"Five fathom and a half," he said, to nobody. "Good water for a stair."

Atticus had not stopped eating. "That was the lead," he said, in his slow voice. Nobody asked him which he meant.

Pell came back with the old boathook, which had half a hook, and bent a line to it. It took him four casts. On the fifth he found the chain between the ankles, and he and two of the Ford drew the man up by it, feet first, hand over hand, and laid him on the bottom step with the sea running out of him.

There was weed in his hair, across his face, in the irons. He was a long time dead; she could see it from the landing. She went down anyway and knelt on the wet stone beside him, because it was what she did, and laid two fingers under the angle of his jaw and waited the proper time, and a little longer, because that was what she did too. She took the weed off his mouth. His eyes were half open. She closed them.

"He's dead," she said.

"Ah," said Rufo, from the water-gate. He sounded sorry, in a general way. When the shield-man came back down at last with the long pole, Rufo sent him straight up again with it, to the cave.

"Tell the prince," he said. "One of the ten. Slipped, on the weed, in his irons." He considered. "Tell him we were very quick."

"Very quick, Centurion."

"Very."

Pell said she sat right now. Nerva said, "Then load her," and the line moved. Two of the nine set down their loads and carried the dead man up the stair between them by his arms and his chain, and the rest went on down past them with the casks, and nobody said anything at all.

Liora got up off the weed, and she was smiling.

At all of them. At the woman who had folded her arms the other way, at Nerva's thirty years of ash, at Baebius with his rag of cloak and Fingers with his odds and Gaius with his five fathom and a half. Not one of them knew there was anything on that stair to give her, and every one of them had given it, at once, without a word passing between them. They would have done as much for any of the ten. That was the joke of it, and the smile widened.

Then she caught it, there on her own mouth, and put her fingers to it.

She waited for the horror. She knew how it came; she had watched it come to a great many men on the pallets at the Baths, the night after their first. It came up out of the stomach, and then the hands began, and then they wanted to tell you about it and you let them. She stood at the foot of the water-gate stair with her fingers on her mouth and waited for hers. Her hands were steady. Her stomach was quiet. Nothing came down the stair to find her but the cold off the Grey.

That was what horrified her, when she understood it. Not the man in the water. That she was not horrified at all.

---

She finished the satchels by feel after the light went. The pot of liniment she put in her own, with the things that went. It would keep.

When she climbed, the tide had turned, and the sea had given back two steps below the one where they had laid him. She stopped in the water-gate, where she had stood every night since in her head and never till today on her feet, and looked down.

They used to call her an angel.

A poet at her father's table, the winter she was fifteen, had likened her, in eleven couplets, to the angels of the Returning Sun on the chapel ceiling at home, gold-haired, carrying the dead up out of the dark; he rhymed her with the dawn, and her father paid him, and she was ashamed for a month. At the Baths, Manius Varenus, burned across the back, had made a song of it to a marching tune, *The Angel of the Baths*, and taught it to the whole ward to make her blush; and when she would not blush they sang it louder. And on the hospital ship coming south a boy of the Fourteenth, opened at Hollowmere, had woken in the lamplight and seen her hair, and asked her quite politely whether she had come for him. She told him no, she had come to change the dressing. He died before the morning watch. She had never known whether he believed her.

That had been before. At the vat she had laid her hand on the copper and said eighty names into it, and something had gone out of her there that she had not known was holding anything up. Last night her clean linen had killed a boy on the bottom step of this stair. Merula had said so, and Merula counted.

In the courtyard she had asked the prince for eleven lives, and on the slip she had told the captain of the Ford to lay them at her door. The Tharsians had taken one back off the bottom step with a crossbow, because of her linen. She had taken one back herself, with his own name. That left nine at her door. Two of the nine she had not decided anything about. She had not decided about him either, until the moment came and stood in front of her on the weed with a keg on its shoulder. In the morning she would sail, and they would not.

She went over it once more, carefully, joint by joint, as she went over a man who had fallen, pressing, asking where it hurt. She had murdered a man. It was her first. The Vigil taught that a name made a man a patient; nobody had ever told her that a name would work the other way as well, and show you where to put the knife. She had found that out for herself, on the landing, in the time it takes to count three. And it did not hurt anywhere. It had been fine. If she was honest, and she was always honest at this point in the order, it had been surprisingly fine.

Behind her, in the hall, over the wine, a man would be telling how very quick they had all been.

*Not a lady.* The cohort said it for her now, in chorus, whenever anyone forgot. Liora had heard Alana say it herself once: that morning in the steam-box house, when the ship was in, with Hostus bent over her cheekbone and his curved needle. *Hold still, my lady.* *Not a lady,* Alana had said, without moving her face. *And I am holding still. It's your needle that moves. There's a difference.* Liora, at the next pallet, had liked her very much for it, and had not said so.

She looked down past the stair's foot at the black water by the sea-door. There was no ring on it now.

She said it to herself, low, in the old vowels, at the pace of the names.

*Not an angel.*

---

**VALERIA**

She had the report in her hand, sealed, and nowhere to put it down.

She stood on the seaward wall with it in the dark, because the hall was full of men drinking the last of the Tharsian wine and the courtyard was full of men packing, and the wall was the only place in Cyrene where nobody would ask her where the spare bill-hafts were. The Grey lay beyond the moles, faintly yellow, like a lamp behind a curtain. Below, the *Forward* lay against the water-gate stair with the engineer's lantern going up and down her rudder-post like a slow firefly.

Six lines. She had written longer reports about a lost tent.

Baebius had come up at dusk to tell her that the big prisoner with the bad back had gone off the foot of that stair in his irons, and that everyone had been very quick; she had looked at Baebius's face for a while, and had not broken the seal.

She heard him on the stair a long time before he came out onto the wall. One step, and a pause, and a breath let out through the nose. Another step. A man coming up a stair with a back like a cracked mast, doing it alone on purpose so that nobody would see him need a hand.

"If you've come up here to fall off," said Valeria, "fall off the sea side. I'm not carrying you down those steps."

"I came up to look at the sea."

"It's dark."

"It's still there. I checked." He leaned on the parapet two paces from her, both forearms on the stone. "The boy stood just there, the night we took the fort. I told him a great many wise things. I can't remember one of them."

"Then they were wise."

He laughed under his breath, and it went into his back, and he stopped.

Below them the surf breathed against the foot of the stair. Somewhere in the town a dog was barking at nothing, on and on, the way dogs do on the first night a town feels safe enough to let them.

"You said a thing on the beach," he said at last. "*I know when it went.* I've not asked."

"I know you haven't." She turned the report over in her hands. "I'll tell you anyway. We sail in the morning. I'd sooner you heard it from me than from a song."

---

She told it the way she read a writ: flat, one thing after another, nothing leaned on.

"My father was at the Long Field. A spearman of the levy, from Callow, in the hills behind Vessa. He cut thorn for a living. He'd never been off the hill." She looked at the Grey, not at him. "You know what the levy was for, that day."

"I know."

"Say it."

He was quiet a moment. "To stand in the middle of the field where the Tharsian heavy horse could see them. And hold. So the horse would keep coming at them, and not look round. So that we could take them in the flank."

"They held," said Valeria. "All the morning. Three charges, with hill spears and no shields to speak of, and the line shorter every time. They held till you came round the wood." She kept her voice where it was. "I was nineteen. The levy took no women, so I came down with the baggage, and sat on a cart behind the camp all that morning, listening to it. My mother came down the next day, and she and I walked the field. Every family from the hills was walking it. We found him under a horse. A big black, with his spear still in its chest. He'd brought it down, and it had come down on him."

The dog in the town had stopped. She did not know when.

"The day before, they'd carried the wounded knights back past my cart, up the road to the camp, on doors and hurdles. One of them was a man not long past thirty, with a halberd-hole under his plate, low on the left, and his horse dead on top of him an hour before. He swore the whole length of the road, and very well, I thought, for a lord." She turned her head and looked at him. "That's how I know when it went."

Roderic said nothing. His hands had closed on the edge of the parapet.

"The spring after, a harper came through Callow with a new song. *The Ride of the Long Field.* All about the charge round the wood. It had your name in it four times. They sang it at the lambing feast, and my mother got up and went out of the room. She never said a word against it. She left the room every time anybody sang it, for nine years, till she died." She looked down at the report. "I took his spear home off the field. I'd never held one in my life. I carried it up the hill to Callow on my shoulder, and my hands were raw with it by the top. The ash of it is on the Farrier now. I've been carrying it nineteen years."

---

She waited for the apology. She had a reply for it. She had had one ready since the first time she heard the song.

It did not come.

He looked down at the dark water so long she began to think he would not speak at all.

"What was his name?"

"Vibius."

"Vibius," he said. "Of Callow." He said it slowly, as the prince said a man's name back to him, as if fixing it somewhere it could not be lost. Then he looked at her, and his face in the yellow of the Grey was old, and grey with no sleep, and there was nothing in it of the man who had come down the beach at her hedge, roaring.

"They held," said Roderic. "That's why we could charge. Every man who rode round that wood knew it. Nobody sings that."

"No," said Valeria. "Nobody does."

"I've been listening nineteen years for someone to."

She had no reply for that. In nineteen years it had not occurred to her she would need one. Her eyes had gone hot, which they had not done since she buried her mother, and she turned her face to the Grey so the wind off it would deal with them.

He did not look. He looked at the harbor and gave her the time, as a man gives a horse its head on a bad slope.

---

"You still charged us," she said, when she could. "On the beach. Shouting it. *The Long Field and the king.* At the Ford."

"I didn't know." He thought about it. "That's not true. I knew there was something. I'll not shout it again."

"You will. It's the only song you know." She looked at him sidelong. "If you ever ride at me in earnest, General, I'll unshoe you."

"I'd expect nothing less."

"And the horse."

"Thistle would never forgive you."

"Thistle," said Valeria, "can take it up with the Farrier."

He laughed, and this time he let it hurt, and did not stop.

She watched him laugh. Fifty-two. A back like a cracked mast and a bad knee and nine wounds, and he would tell anyone the number. Two wives buried; the Ford had told her that on the *Patience*, idly, over dice, the way soldiers tell you everything you have not asked. A man who had ridden round a wood while her father died holding the middle of the field, and had heard his own name sung four times over ever since. He was everything she had spent her life learning to stop.

She found she did not care.

Habit reached for the list. Under the other heading, then, with the rest, to be dealt with later. Later was a country she had never been posted to.

There would be time, or there would not. She had been a soldier long enough to know the second was likelier. In the morning the *Forward* would go into the Grey with twelve flasks of Lucan's poison in her hold, toward the elves, who had every reason to shoot first. Roderic had said it himself in the cave, with his head against the pillar. *We walk into their wood carrying their poison.* Every soldier learned the rule in his first campaign or did not live to learn it in his second. Eat when there is bread. Sleep when there is a dry floor. Bread kept for after the battle goes to the crows. She had buried a great many men with something saved for after.

She was done saving.

"Roderic."

"Captain."

"I keep a list. Nineteen years. Things that might kill me." She watched the Grey while she said it. "There's a second heading. I put things under it to deal with later."

"And do you?"

"Never once."

Below them Gaius's lantern stopped on the rudder-post and hung there, and a voice swore at a pintle, clearly, across the water.

"I was about to put you under it," said Valeria.

He was quiet a while. When he spoke it was not the voice he used in the wine-halls.

"There's a day at the end of winter," he said, "when the wind goes round and the ground gives under your boot. A soft day. Every farmer knows it, and knows what comes after. Mud, mostly." He turned his head and looked at her. "I've been wary of them a long while. Two graves' worth. Then you put me on my back in the sea, and afterward I stood in it with a towel round my neck and thought: Roderic, you are fifty-two years old, and you do not get so many of these that you can send one back to the kitchen."

"I'm not soft."

"No. I noticed. Twice."

"Three times, if your horse had any ambition." She turned the report over once more. "Mud, mostly."

"Mostly."

"Terms, then."

He straightened as far as his back would go, which was not far. "Let's hear them."

"Tonight. Not after. There may not be an after, and I'll not be cheated out of it by a wood full of archers."

"Agreed."

"No songs."

"I'll drown the harper."

"And you'll not kneel."

"Sunstone be thanked. I'd never get up."

"I know. I'd have to carry you, and I said I wouldn't." She considered. "That's all."

"Mine, then." He held up one thick finger. "I snore. Felix says it's like a siege train going over a bridge. That's not a term. It's a warning, given in fair time."

"I've slept in a ditch with sixty of the Ford."

"None of them was me."

"No," said Valeria. "That's the point."

She shifted the report to her left hand, since there was still nowhere to put it down, and took a fistful of his collar in her right, and kissed him.

His beard had been combed. She noticed that first, and nearly laughed against his mouth: he had come up in the dark to look at the sea with his beard combed. He tasted of the Tharsian wine. He did not hurry. He had never hurried anything in his life but a charge, and it seemed he had learned something since the Long Field. His hand came up to the left side of her face and lay along the scar, eye to jaw, warm, and stayed. Then his other arm came round her, mail and all, and drew her in, and it stopped being careful. The report crackled between them, and she did not care about that either. She had kept a reply ready for him for nineteen years. This was not it. She found she had nothing ready at all, and did not miss it.

When they came apart the report was crushed flat between them, and the seal had printed itself into her palm.

He looked down at it. "They'll have your captaincy for that."

"Probably," said Valeria.

She put it inside her mail against her ribs, where she had carried the king's writ, and drew the strap tight over it.

He pushed himself off the parapet and stood a moment getting his back under him, and turned for the stairhead alone, as he had come up.

Valeria came beside him at the top step, took his arm, and put it over her shoulders.

"I can manage a stair."

"I know. I heard you manage it. A step and a breath, all the way up." She settled her feet. "I said I'd not carry you. I never said I'd not hold you up. Anchor on me."

He looked at her a long moment. Then he let the weight come, and it was more than she had guessed, a great deal more: a lifetime of armor and horses and bad landings, all of it held up by one cracked back, alone, on purpose. She took it square, without a step back, as the Ford takes a horse.

They went down together. Below them the surf breathed against the foot of the water-gate stair, and Gaius's lantern had started up and down the *Forward*'s rudder-post again, and somewhere in the town the dog had found something new to bark at. The Grey lay beyond the moles, faintly yellow. It would still be there in the morning.

The bill-hafts could wait.

---

**ALANA**

The chair took up half the room.

Marcus had set it down by the window-slit two nights ago, out of breath, and gone down the stair again without a word. It was keel stock. Tonight she sat in it with the weather-book on her knee and the lamp on the sill, and the trunk at the foot of the cot, and the little carved woman on the top rail flinging up her arms over her head; and she could not write the weather. *Wind, none. The Grey thick to the gap, yellow at the*—and there it stopped.

She turned to the back instead.

She had done it every night for two years, in the lodging-house and in the stern cabin of the *Clemency* and in this room: turned to the back, where the dead were, and read them down. Master Hesper, who had taught her to stand. The boy who trimmed the lamps in the long reading room, whose name she had never thought to ask, written there as *the lamp-boy*. The harbor, in one line, because there had been too many to write one by one, standing in their rows. Then the cohort's dead, as many as she had names for; and at the bottom of the last page, by itself, in a hand gone loose with the Tharsian wine, the name she had put there the night of the beach. Livia she did last, without writing, because Livia was alive and wrote letters.

She tried to remember when she had last done it.

Not last night; last night she had not slept. Not the night before, when she had sat in this chair scarlet to the hair and thought of nothing in the world but how the joints were pegged. Not the night of the drill, when she had laughed herself sick at the General in the surf. Not since the still-room.

She waited for the old weight to come down. It did not come. She read them down again from the top, slowly, and it still did not come; and through the window-slit, far off, the cohort on the black beach was murdering a song.

And there had been the steam-box house, in the small hours, with the blood still drying under her nose: his arm laid open on the table under somebody else's hands, and the honey-gold head bent over it, and the face half the island had told her, unasked, was the loveliest it had ever seen, quite calm. Alana had stood in the doorway with the snow at her back and found out how much she minded. Then she had gone back up the slip. She had not written that down.

On the beach he had offered, and she had said *not tonight*, and he had not asked again. He would not. He had kept the hand's breadth off her ever since, kept it exactly, because it was the one thing left he could give her that he had not already thrown away. He would keep it to the wood, and into the ground, if she let him.

It was not his to close. It had not been since the beach.

She put the book inside her robe against her ribs, and left the lamp burning, and went down.

The courtyard was full of men packing, and Felix in the middle of them with a lantern and a list, the only man in Cyrene with a longer list than hers. The hall roared. He would not be in there. On the seaward wall a shape in mail stood alone against the yellow of the Grey, and from the foot of the wall-stair came a slow tread going up: a step, and a breath, and a step.

Alana went the other way.

The town had come down to the fire on the black beach, which it had not done the first time. Fishwives in their shawls sat among the cohort as if they always had, and Tam sat on an upturned basket beside Kaeso with his mouth open, being shown the drum. She did not go into the light. She went round the back of the dunes in the dark, with the warm sand giving under her boots, to the place where the marram ended above the fire, stiff with old snow.

He was there. She had known he would be. *From the dunes,* he had said. *Since the drum.* He stood at the edge of the dark with his cloak round him, watching the fire and not going down to it, and in the light that came up the slope she could see his hands shaking at his sides. Nothing to fight, and a whole night of it.

"Alana," he said, without turning round.

So he knew her step too. She had not known that.

She came and stood beside him, a hand's breadth off. It was a hard habit. Below them the cohort was singing the song about the girl in Vessa with the wooden leg, over the objections of the Vessans, and the fishwives were singing it with them, with additions.

"I haven't grieved in three days," she said. "Four, by the morning."

He turned his head.

"For Corvus. The masters. The library. The harbor." She kept her eyes on the fire. "Two years I've done it every night, in the back of the book, in order. And Livia after, who isn't dead, which is worse. I'm very thorough. And now I keep forgetting to be sad."

He did not answer at once.

"Does it frighten you?"

She considered it exactly, because he had asked it exactly. "Yes. And it doesn't. That's the part that frightens me."

"I know that one," said Marcus.

"I came here to die, Highness. I'd like that understood. Not bravely; I've never seen the use. Before we sailed I asked a chandler on the quay how much oil it takes to burn a man so nobody can get him up again, and multiplied, and wrote it down. Your father sent two hundred thieves and a son in disgrace, and I went with them to see that something burned before we did. Everybody on that ship knew what they were for. I'd made my peace with it. I'd ruled a line under it."

"And now?"

"And now there's more. Since the table on the beach. Every day there's been more than the debt and the dying, and I don't know what to do with it, and tomorrow we take twelve flasks of that black into the Grey, to a wood full of people who'll shoot us before we've a foot on the sand. Valeria says so. The General didn't argue. You said you would."

"I would."

"So the time may be short." She turned and looked at him at last. "And I have always hated waste."
